Lesson 3. Two-step rules, odd/even, missing and wrong terms

In one line: the gaps of a "×a ± b" series get multiplied by a, which reveals the rule in seconds.

1Picture this

A chaiwala in Kanpur sells 3 cups in the first hour, 5 in the second. Next hour 6, then 10. Two things are happening at once: the odd hours go 3, 6, 9 and the even hours go 5, 10, 15. Looking at the whole list is confusing. Looking at two lists is easy.

3 6 9 12
5 10 15 20

When the gaps jump up and down, split into odd and even positions.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Some rules have two steps: multiply, then add or subtract. Example: ×2 + 1 turns 3 into 7, then 7 into 15.
Hidden trick: for a rule "×a ± b", the gaps themselves get multiplied by a. In 3, 7, 15, 31 the gaps are 4, 8, 16.
So: if the gaps double, the rule is ×2 ± something. If the gaps triple, ×3 ± something.
Find the added number b: take the first term, apply ×a, and see what is needed to reach the second term.
If gaps jump up and down, split the series into odd and even positions and treat each as its own small series.
Missing term: find the rule from the side with more terms, then fill the blank. Example 4, 10, ?, 82, 244: 244 ÷ 82 is just under 3, so try ×3 − b. 4 × 3 = 12 but the next term is 10, so b = 2 (×3 − 2). Check: 82 × 3 − 2 = 244.
Wrong term: find the rule from the terms that agree with each other, then spot the one that breaks it.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Here t is any term, a is what you multiply by, b is what you add (or subtract). Rule: t(next) = a × t + b. Then each gap = a × the previous gap.
Find a = (second gap) ÷ (first gap). Then b = second term − a × first term.
Wrong-term: the terms after the wrong one follow the rule from the true value.
The routine, same order every time: 1) gaps. 2) gaps of the gaps. 3) ratios. 4) squares and cubes. 5) primes, or sum of previous two. 6) odd and even positions apart. 7) × a, then ± b.

Why it works: t₃−t₂ = (a·t₂+b) − (a·t₁+b) = a(t₂−t₁). Checked by Python for a from −3 to 5, b from −6 to 6. The 7-step order is our own suggested routine.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Find the next term: 3, 7, 15, 31, 63, ?

Step 1. Gaps: 4, 8, 16, 32. They double, so a = 2.
Step 2. b = 7 − 2×3 = 1. Rule: ×2 + 1.
Step 3. Check: 15×2+1 = 31, 31×2+1 = 63.
Step 4. Next: 63×2 + 1 = 127.

Find the next term: 3, 5, 6, 10, 9, 15, 12, 20, ?

Step 1. Gaps jump: 2, 1, 4, −1, 6, −3, 8. Split by position: odd positions are the 1st, 3rd, 5th, 7th terms; even positions are the 2nd, 4th, 6th, 8th.
Step 2. Odd positions: 3, 6, 9, 12. Add 3 each time.
Step 3. Even positions: 5, 10, 15, 20. Add 5 each time.
Step 4. The blank is the 9th term, an odd position: 12 + 3 = 15.

Find the wrong term: 5, 11, 23, 48, 95, 191

Step 1. Gaps 6, 12 double, so try ×2 + b. 5×2 + 1 = 11 and 11×2 + 1 = 23, so the rule is ×2 + 1.
Step 2. 23×2+1 = 47, but the series says 48. So 48 is wrong.
Step 3. Check: 47×2+1 = 95 and 95×2+1 = 191. Both match the true 47. Wrong term: 48 (should be 47).

5The trap

Series: 2, 5, 11, 20, ?
Wrong move: "2→5 is ×2+1, 5→11 is ×2+1, so next is ×2+1". That gives 20 × 2 + 1 = 41.
But 11 × 2 + 1 = 23, not 20. The rule broke at the third step.
Right: gaps are 3, 6, 9. Next gap 12, answer 20 + 12 = 32.
Test every pair before you trust the rule.

6Mental-math shortcut: gap ratio finds the rule

Write the first three gaps. Divide the second by the first. That number is a. Then b falls out.

SeriesGapsab = t2 − a·t1 (t1, t2 = first and second terms)
3, 7, 15, 314, 8, 1621
2, 8, 26, 806, 18, 5432
4, 10, 28, 826, 18, 543−2

Speed drill

Say the rule out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: rule builder

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "Find the missing term: 4, 10, ?, 82, 244"
  • "One number is wrong in the series 5, 11, 23, 48, 95, 191. Find it."
  • "What comes next: 3, 6, 5, 12, 7, 24, 9, ?"

Number series was reported as 5 questions in 2022 in memory-based exam analyses. Which sub-types were asked is not itemised there. This lesson covers the harder forms, so you are ready for any.

How the wrong options are built. Wrong options come from using only ×a (missing the ±b), or repeating the last gap. Compute the rule fully before you look at the options.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%. Cross out options first.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Gaps double: what is a? (2) How do I get b? (3) Gaps jump up and down: what do I do? (4) How do I spot the wrong term?
Memorise this
1. Gaps get multiplied by a.
2. a = second gap ÷ first gap.
3. b = t2 − a × t1.
4. Zig-zag gaps: split odd and even positions.
5. Check every pair, then answer.

Sources: Gap rule for ×a ± b: derived, Python-checked; AP/GP definitions as in lesson 1. Past-paper count: edutap exam analysis (memory-based).

Quadratic Equations (x and y comparison)

In one line: solve two small quadratics, list the roots, and compare x with y. Reported as 5 questions in 2025 in memory-based exam analyses, so this chapter is worth the effort.

Lesson 1 · Solve one quadratic

In one line: a quadratic is solved by finding two numbers that multiply to the last term and add to the middle one, then flipping their signs to get the roots.

1Picture this

At the hostel mess a friend sets you a puzzle: “two numbers multiply to 12 and add to 7”. You think: 3 and 4. A quadratic equation is that same puzzle in disguise.

3 × 4= 12

Product 12, sum 7. Factor-pair thinking is the whole engine of this chapter.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

A quadratic equation is one where the highest power of x is x² (see chapter 11 for powers). Standard form: ax² + bx + c = 0, where a, b, c are plain numbers and a is not zero. A term is one chunk of the equation, like ax² or bx or c.
It usually has two answers for x. They are called roots. A "real" root is an ordinary number you can put on a number line.
Look at x² − 7x + 12 = 0. Find two numbers that multiply to 12 and add to −7. Those are −3 and −4.
Write it as (x − 3)(x − 4) = 0. A product is zero only if one part is zero.
So x − 3 = 0 or x − 4 = 0. Roots: x = 3 and x = 4. Note: the roots have the OPPOSITE sign to the numbers inside the brackets.
If the x² has a number in front (like 2x²), multiply a by c first and hunt for a pair with that product (worked in example 2 below). That is the “split the middle term” method.
Untidy equation? Move everything to one side. x² = 5x − 6: subtract 5x and add 6 on both sides, giving x² − 5x + 6 = 0. With brackets, expand first: (x + 3)(x − 2) = 6 becomes x² − 2x + 3x − 6 = 6, so x² + x − 6 = 6, so x² + x − 12 = 0.
Quick check on any answer: the two roots add up to −b/a and multiply to c/a. For x² − 7x + 12 (a = 1): 3 + 4 = 7 and 3 × 4 = 12. If a is not 1, divide the whole equation by a first (3x² − 12x + 9 = 0 becomes x² − 4x + 3 = 0).

3The rules (now that you get it)

Standard form: ax² + bx + c = 0
Split the middle: find two numbers with product = a × c and sum = b. Rewrite bx using them, group, factor.
Zero-product rule: if (px + q)(rx + s) = 0, then px + q = 0 or rx + s = 0.
Sign rule for x² + bx + c (a = 1, real roots): c positive → both roots have the SAME sign, the opposite of b’s sign. c negative → roots have DIFFERENT signs; the root that is bigger (ignoring its sign) has the opposite sign of b.

Standard form, factoring (AC method) and zero-product: Math Is Fun pages in Sources. Sign rule: derived, Python-checked (all integer root pairs from −20 to 20).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Solve x² − 7x + 12 = 0.

Step 1. a = 1, b = −7, c = 12. Need product 12 and sum −7.
Step 2. Pairs of 12: (1,12), (2,6), (3,4). Signs: c is positive so both same sign; b is negative so both negative: −3 and −4 (sum −7).
Step 3. Split: x² − 3x − 4x + 12. Take x out of the first two terms and −4 out of the last two: x(x − 3) − 4(x − 3). Both leave (x − 3), so it is (x − 3)(x − 4).
Step 4. Roots: x = 3 and x = 4. Check: 3 + 4 = 7 = −b, 3 × 4 = 12 = c.

Solve 2x² + 7x + 3 = 0.

Step 1. a = 2, b = 7, c = 3. Product a × c = 6, sum must be 7.
Step 2. Pairs of 6: (1,6) adds to 7. Good.
Step 3. Split: 2x² + x + 6x + 3. Take x out of the first two terms and 3 out of the last two: x(2x + 1) + 3(2x + 1). Both leave (2x + 1), so it is (2x + 1)(x + 3).
Step 4. 2x + 1 = 0: take 1 across to get 2x = −1, so x = −1/2. x + 3 = 0 gives x = −3. Answer: −1/2 and −3.

5The trap

Equation: x² − 7x + 12 = 0. Factors: (x − 3)(x − 4).
Wrong move: copy the numbers inside the brackets with their signs and write the roots as −3 and −4.
Test x = −3: (−3)² − 7(−3) + 12 = 9 + 21 + 12 = 42, not 0. So −3 is wrong.
Right: (x − 3) = 0 means x = +3. Roots are the numbers that make a bracket zero: opposite sign to the number inside. Always substitute one root to check.

6Mental-math shortcut: factor pairs and the sign rule

Learn the pairs of c once. Then you only look for the pair whose sum is |b| (b with its sign ignored, so |−7| = 7).

ProductPairs (sum)
121,12 (13) · 2,6 (8) · 3,4 (7)
201,20 (21) · 2,10 (12) · 4,5 (9)
241,24 (25) · 2,12 (14) · 3,8 (11) · 4,6 (10)
301,30 (31) · 2,15 (17) · 3,10 (13) · 5,6 (11)
361,36 (37) · 2,18 (20) · 3,12 (15) · 4,9 (13) · 6,6 (12)
Sign routine (for the ROOTS). (1) c positive: same sign, take the opposite of b’s sign. (2) c negative: one + one −; the bigger number gets the opposite sign to b. Example x² + 2x − 15: pair 3,5 differ by 2; bigger (5) gets the sign opposite to +2, so −5 and +3.

Speed drill

Say the two numbers aloud before you tap Show. These are the pair numbers (sum = b); the roots are the same numbers with signs flipped.

7Lab: factor-pair finder

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • “Solve: x² − 9x + 20 = 0.” (find both roots)
  • “I. 2x² + 7x + 3 = 0   II. y² + 4y + 3 = 0. Compare x and y.” Here Lesson 1 gives the roots; Lesson 2 does the comparison.
  • Equations are often given in untidy form, like x² = 5x − 6 or (x + 2)(x − 1) = 4. First move everything to one side so it equals 0.
Reported, not official. Quadratic Equations were reported as 5 of the 20 Quant questions in the 2025 paper in memory-based exam analyses (edutap.in). The official notification lists no Quant topics. Five questions = 6.25 of 25 Quant marks.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%: 0.3125 ÷ (1.25 + 0.3125) = 0.2.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What two numbers do I hunt for? (2) Do roots keep the sign of the factor or flip it? (3) What does c positive tell me about the roots? (4) What do I do when a is not 1?
Memorise this
1. Move everything to one side: ax² + bx + c = 0.
2. Find two numbers: product = a×c, sum = b.
3. Roots make each bracket zero: flip the sign inside the bracket.
4. c > 0: same sign roots (opposite of b). c < 0: mixed signs.
5. Check: sum of roots = −b/a, product = c/a.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Quadratic Equations · Math Is Fun: Factoring Quadratics · Wikipedia: Quadratic equation · 2025 count: edutap.in/pfrda-grade-a/exam-analysis/ (memory-based). Sign rule: derived, Python-checked. All worked numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: the exact form of the 2025 quadratic questions; none else.

Lesson 2 · The x vs y relation grid

In one line: solve both equations, put the four roots on a number line, and compare the x roots against the y roots one pair at a time.

1Picture this

Two teams, X and Y, each have two players with heights in cm. The coach asks: “is X taller than Y?” You can only say yes if EVERY X player is taller than EVERY Y player. If the tallest of Y stands between X players, you cannot say.

x roots: 2, 3y roots: 1, 2

Here the shortest X (2) ties with the tallest Y (2). That tie is why the answer is “≥”, not “>”. (≥ means “greater than or equal to”, ≤ means “less than or equal to”.)

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Question type: two equations, one in x and one in y. Each gives two values (usually).
Step A: solve the x equation. Write the two x values, small to large.
Step B: solve the y equation. Write the two y values, small to large.
Step C: min means smallest and max means largest. Compare the SMALLEST x with the LARGEST y, and the LARGEST x with the SMALLEST y. Only these two checks decide everything.
Step D: if smallest x is above largest y, every x beats every y: x > y. If largest x is below smallest y: x < y. If both a strict and a loose statement are true (x > y and x ≥ y), give the strict one: it is the most exact.
Step E: if they only touch (equal) and nothing crosses, use ≥ or ≤. If the x values and y values overlap or interleave (some x below a y and some x above it, like x = −3, 2 and y = −1, 3), there is no relation.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Let x have roots in X, y in Y. Compare min X, max X with min Y, max Y.
x > y if min X > max Y  ·  x < y if max X < min Y
(“Strictly >” means greater and not equal.)
x ≥ y if min X ≥ max Y (and not strictly >)  ·  x ≤ y if max X ≤ min Y (and not strictly <)
Otherwise: no relation. If every root of both is the same single value, x = y.

This is a logical definition: “x > y” means true for every pair. Derived, Python-checked against the pair-by-pair definition on 20,000 random root sets.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

I. x² − 5x + 6 = 0   II. y² − 3y + 2 = 0

Step 1. x: pair with product 6, sum −5 is −2, −3. (x − 2)(x − 3) = 0. x = 2, 3.
Step 2. y: product 2, sum −3 is −1, −2. (y − 1)(y − 2) = 0. y = 1, 2.
Step 3. Smallest x = 2, largest y = 2. Equal, so not strictly greater. Largest x = 3, smallest y = 1: x not below y.
Step 4. Every x is at least every y: x ≥ y.

I. x² + x − 6 = 0   II. y² − 2y − 3 = 0

Step 1. x: product −6, sum +1: 3 and −2 give sum +1, so factors (x + 3)(x − 2). x = −3, 2.
Step 2. y: product −3, sum −2: the numbers are −3 and +1. (y − 3)(y + 1) = 0. y = 3, −1.
Step 3. Smallest x = −3 is below biggest y = 3. Biggest x = 2 is above smallest y = −1. Both directions occur.
Step 4. Answer: no relation (x = y or cannot be established).

5The trap

Equations: x² − 5x + 6 = 0 (x = 2, 3) and y² − 3y + 2 = 0 (y = 1, 2).
Wrong move: pair them in order. Biggest with biggest: 3 > 2. Smallest with smallest: 2 > 1. Conclude x > y.
That ignores the pair x = 2 and y = 2. They are equal, so “x > y” fails for that pair.
Right: check ALL four pairs (or just min X vs max Y). The answer is x ≥ y.

6Mental-math shortcut: min-vs-max routine and practice (10 items)

The 2-check routine. Write X and Y small to large. Compare (min X, max Y) and (max X, min Y). Then use this grid:
min X vs max Ymax X vs min YAnswer
min X > max Y(automatically above)x > y
min X = max Ymax X above min Yx ≥ y
(automatically below)max X < min Yx < y
min X below max Ymax X = min Yx ≤ y
min X below max Ymax X above min Yno relation

Practice drill: 10 pairs

Solve on paper first, then tap Show answer to check roots and relation. In the typical style, the last option is “x = y or no relation”.

7Lab: relation grid solver

Type the numbers a, b, c of each equation (a x² + b x + c = 0). Try a = 0 for a simple linear equation like 2y + 3 = 0.

Equation I (x) Equation II (y)

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • “I. x² − 7x + 12 = 0   II. y² − 5y + 4 = 0. Give answer: (A) x > y (B) x < y (C) x ≥ y (D) x ≤ y (E) x = y or the relation cannot be established.”
  • Sometimes one equation is linear, like 2y + 3 = 0, giving a single y value.
  • Sometimes the equation needs tidying first: x² = 5x − 6 becomes x² − 5x + 6 = 0.
Reported, not official. 5 Quadratic Equation questions were reported for 2025 in memory-based exam analyses (edutap.in). The source I used does not say how those questions were worded or what the options were. A third-party topic list (Oliveboard, not official) names “Inequalities (quadratic)”.
How the wrong options are built. “>” and “≥” sit side by side, and so do “<” and “≤”. The difference is one tied pair of roots. Always check the touching pair.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%: 0.3125 ÷ (1.25 + 0.3125) = 0.2.

Options use the typical five-option style (the last option is “x = y or no relation”, shown in a varied position here).

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Which two comparisons decide the answer? (2) When is it ≥ instead of >? (3) When is there no relation? (4) Why must I list all roots first?
Memorise this
1. Solve both. List roots small to large.
2. min X vs max Y; max X vs min Y.
3. min X > max Y: x > y. max X < min Y: x < y.
4. Touching only: ≥ or ≤.
5. Overlap or interleave: no relation.

Sources: definitions of roots, factoring and zero-product: Math Is Fun pages as in Lesson 1; comparison routine: derived, Python-checked (20,000 random root sets, plus 10 drill pairs and 5 quiz pairs solved by code). 2025 count: edutap.in/pfrda-grade-a/exam-analysis/ (memory-based).
UNVERIFIED: the wording/options of the real 2025 questions; whether the real option set matches the typical five-option style.

Lesson 3 · Discriminant and shortcuts

In one line: the number b² − 4ac tells you how many real roots there are before you solve, and sum and product of roots let you check or skip the work.

1Picture this

Before a long train journey you check the ticket status: confirmed, waitlisted, or cancelled. The discriminant does the same for a quadratic: two real answers, one repeated answer, or none. It costs 10 seconds and saves you from chasing roots that do not exist.

D > 0: twoD = 0: oneD < 0: none

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

For ax² + bx + c = 0, compute D = b² − 4ac. Call it the discriminant.
If D is positive, there are two different real roots.
If D is zero, there is exactly one real root, repeated (it equals −b/2a). In the x-vs-y question, that equation gives ONE number.
If D is negative, there are no real roots. (These are not used in the x-vs-y comparison.)
Many k-questions ask “for what k are the roots equal (repeated)?” Set D = 0 and solve for k. Example x² − 6x + k = 0: D = 36 − 4k = 0, so k = 9.
If D is a perfect square (a number whose root is whole: 1, 4, 9, 16, 25 ...) and a, b, c are whole numbers, the roots are fractions or whole numbers that factor neatly.
Sum of roots = −b/a. Product of roots = c/a. Use them to check your roots in 3 seconds.
Also watch for x² = k (like x² = 49): this has TWO roots, +7 and −7.

3The rules (now that you get it)

D = b² − 4ac   (D > 0: two real roots; D = 0: one repeated; D < 0: none real)
Quadratic formula: x = (−b ± √D) ÷ 2a (± means do a plus once and a minus once: that gives the two roots)
Sum of roots = −b/a  ·  Product of roots = c/a
Build an equation from roots r and s: x² − (r + s)x + rs = 0

Discriminant cases and Vieta’s formulas: Wikipedia “Quadratic equation”; formula and discriminant: Math Is Fun. Building an equation from roots: from a(x − r)(x − s) = 0 on Wikipedia with a = 1; derived, Python-checked.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Find D and the roots of 2x² − 5x + 3 = 0.

Step 1. a = 2, b = −5, c = 3.
Step 2. D = (−5)² − 4 × 2 × 3 = 25 − 24 = 1. Positive and a perfect square: two neat roots.
Step 3. −b = 5, √D = √1 = 1, 2a = 4. So x = (5 ± 1) ÷ 4: (5 + 1) ÷ 4 = 6/4 = 3/2 and (5 − 1) ÷ 4 = 4/4 = 1.
Step 4. Check: sum = 5/2 = −b/a. Product = 3/2 = c/a. Both match.

Build the equation whose roots are 3 and −5.

Step 1. Sum = 3 + (−5) = −2. Product = 3 × (−5) = −15.
Step 2. x² − (sum)x + product = x² − (−2)x + (−15).
Step 3. Answer: x² + 2x − 15 = 0. Check: it matches the Lesson 1 example.

5The trap

Equations: I. x² = 49   II. y − 7 = 0, so y = 7.
Wrong move: take the square root and write only x = 7. Then x = y.
Right: x² = 49 gives x = 7 AND x = −7. Test: (−7)² = 49.
Now x is in {−7, 7}, y = 7. Every x is at most y, but x = −7 is strictly below: answer x ≤ y, not x = y.

6Mental-math shortcut: perfect squares and Vieta, plus practice (8 items)

Squares to know for D. 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36, 49, 64, 81, 100, 121, 144, 169, 196, 225. If D is one of these, roots are neat and factoring works. If not (with whole-number a, b, c), the roots are not whole numbers or fractions: use the formula.
Check by sum and product (called Vieta’s formulas). After finding two roots, add them: must equal −b/a. Multiply them: must equal c/a. 3 seconds, catches sign slips.

Drill 1: discriminant

Drill 2: 8 practice pairs (includes x² = k and linear equations)

Work each on paper, then Show answer.

7Lab: discriminant and roots

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • “I. x² = 49   II. y = 7 (given as y − 7 = 0). Compare x and y.”
  • “For what value of k does x² − 6x + k = 0 have equal roots?”
  • “If the sum of the roots is 5 and the product is 4, find the equation.”
  • Equations in disguise: 3x² + 11x = −6, or (x + 3)(x − 2) = 6 — expand and move to one side first.
Reported, not official. Quadratic equations: 5 questions in 2025, in memory-based exam analyses (edutap.in). Whether discriminant-style questions appeared is not stated there.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%: 0.3125 ÷ (1.25 + 0.3125) = 0.2.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What is D? (2) What does D = 0 mean for x-vs-y? (3) Sum and product of roots? (4) How many roots does x² = 49 have?
Memorise this
1. D = b² − 4ac: >0 two, =0 one, <0 none.
2. Roots: (−b ± √D) ÷ 2a.
3. Sum = −b/a, product = c/a.
4. x² = k gives two roots (±).
5. Routine: tidy → solve both → list small to large → min X vs max Y, max X vs min Y.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Quadratic Equations · Wikipedia: Quadratic equation (discriminant cases, Vieta, a(x − r)(x − s) form). Equation-from-roots: derived, Python-checked. All numbers checked by Python.

Inequalities and Quantity Comparison

Lesson 1: Linear inequalities and the flip rule

In one line: solve it like an equation, but flip the sign whenever you multiply or divide by a negative.

1Picture this

Rohan has ₹500 in his wallet and a bus pass costs ₹60 per trip. "How many trips can he afford?" is not asking for ONE number. It is asking for a whole range: any number of trips up to 8 works (500 ÷ 60 = 8.33, and you cannot take a third of a trip, so 8).

0 to 8 trips: affordable9, 10: no

An inequality is an equation that answers with a range, not a single value.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

x is just a letter for an unknown number. 3x means 3 times x. An inequality says x is bigger or smaller than something, instead of equal to it.
Symbols: > means greater than, < means less than. The small end points at the smaller number.
≥ and ≤ also allow "equal to". x ≥ 3 means x can be 3 or anything bigger.
Plain > and < are "strict": they do NOT allow equal. x > 6 means 6.5 or 7 work, but 6 itself does not. The number where the range starts (the 6) is called the boundary.
An integer is a whole number: positive, zero or negative (−2, 0, 7). "Smallest integer" questions ask for the first whole number that fits.
You can add or subtract the same number on both sides. The sign does not change.
You can multiply or divide both sides by a positive number. The sign does not change.
Multiply or divide by a negative number and the sign flips. Reason: 7 > 3 but −7 < −3.
Solve like an equation, step by step, and keep one eye on the sign every time you meet a minus.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Add or subtract the same number: sign stays.
Multiply or divide by a positive number: sign stays.
Multiply or divide by a negative number: sign FLIPS.
Swapping the two sides also flips: 7 < x is the same as x > 7.

Rules as stated in Math Is Fun "Solving Inequalities" and Wikipedia "Inequality (mathematics)" (see Sources).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Solve 3x − 7 > 11.

Step 1. Add 7 to both sides: 3x > 18.
Step 2. Divide both sides by 3 (positive, so no flip): x > 6.
Step 3. Check with x = 7: 3×7 − 7 = 14, and 14 > 11. True. Answer: x > 6.

Solve 5 − 2x ≤ 13.

Step 1. Subtract 5 from both sides: −2x ≤ 8.
Step 2. Divide both sides by −2. Negative, so flip: x ≥ −4.
Step 3. Check with x = 0: 5 − 0 = 5 and 5 ≤ 13. True. Check with x = −5: 5 + 10 = 15, and 15 ≤ 13 is false, correctly outside. Answer: x ≥ −4.

5The trap

Question: solve −3x < 12.
Wrong move: divide by −3 and keep the sign. That gives x < −4.
Test the wrong answer: x = −5 should work, but −3 × (−5) = 15 and 15 < 12 is false.
Right: dividing by −3 flips the sign, so x > −4. Test x = 0: 0 < 12 is true.

6Mental-math shortcut: test one number

After solving, plug in ONE number from your answer range. If it fails, you probably forgot to flip. It costs about 5 seconds.

Routine. (1) Move the plain number across by adding or subtracting. (2) Look at the number stuck to x. Is it negative? Flip. (3) Divide. (4) Test one value.

Speed drill: flip or stay?

Say the answer before you tap Show. Aim: under 5 seconds each.

7Lab: linear inequality solver

Solves a·x + b (sign) c

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "Solve: 4x − 5 < 19. Which is the correct range for x?"
  • "What is the smallest integer x that satisfies 3x − 4 > 14?"
  • "What is the largest integer x such that 7 − 3x > −5?"

Word clues: "smallest integer" with > means start just ABOVE the boundary. "Largest integer" with < means stop just BELOW it.

How the wrong options are built. The examiner plants the un-flipped answer, the answer with the boundary value included, and the answer where you forgot one step. If one option matches your answer except that the sign points the other way, re-check whether you divided by a negative.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%. Crossing out one option improves the odds.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What happens to the sign when I divide by a negative? (2) Does adding a negative flip it? (3) What does 7 < x become with x first?
Memorise this
1. Add or subtract: no flip.
2. Multiply or divide by positive: no flip.
3. Multiply or divide by NEGATIVE: flip the sign.
4. Swapping sides flips it too.
5. Finish by testing one number from your answer.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Solving Inequalities · Wikipedia: Inequality (mathematics) · Math Is Fun: Inequality symbols · All worked numbers checked by Python. Guess threshold 20% (= 0.3125 ÷ 1.5625): derived, Python-checked.
UNVERIFIED: none

Lesson 2: Quantity I versus Quantity II

In one line: work out each quantity fully, then compare the two numbers; if the comparison can flip, it cannot be determined.

1Picture this

Two mobile plans. Plan I gives 20% off a ₹150 recharge. Plan II gives 30% off a ₹90 recharge. Which discount is bigger in rupees? The bigger percentage does not automatically win.

Plan I: 20% of 150 = ₹30
Plan II: 30% of 90 = ₹27

Compute both numbers first. Then compare.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

A quantity comparison gives you Quantity I and Quantity II. You decide which is bigger, or whether they are equal.
Step A: find the value of Quantity I completely.
Step B: find the value of Quantity II completely.
Step C: put the two numbers side by side and compare.
If a quantity is a range or has two possible values, test the extreme cases. If the comparison flips between cases, the answer is "cannot be determined".
In a fraction a/b, a is the top number and b is the bottom number (the denominator).
To compare two fractions of positive numbers, cross-multiply: a/b versus c/d becomes a×d versus b×c.
x² means x × x. So if x² = 49, x can be 7 or −7, because (−7) × (−7) is also 49 (see chapter 13).
Handy percent forms from chapter 04: 25% = 1/4 and 12.5% = 1/8.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Compute I and II separately, then compare. Never compare half-finished work.
a/b < c/d exactly when a×d < b×c (all numbers positive).
If I > II in one case and I < II in another: cannot be determined.

Cross-multiplying is allowed because multiplying both sides by a positive number keeps the sign (Math Is Fun; Wikipedia, see Sources). Python checked the rule on every a, b, c, d from 1 to 12.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

I: 25% of 240. II: 3/8 of 160. Compare.

Step 1. I: 25% = 1/4, and 240 ÷ 4 = 60.
Step 2. II: 160 ÷ 8 = 20, then 20 × 3 = 60.
Step 3. 60 and 60. Answer: I = II.

I: x if 3x + 5 = 20. II: y if 2y − 1 = 11. Compare.

Step 1. I: subtract 5 from both sides: 3x = 15. Divide by 3: x = 5.
Step 2. II: add 1 to both sides: 2y = 12. Divide by 2: y = 6.
Step 3. 5 < 6. Answer: I < II.

5The trap

I: 20% of 150. II: 30% of 90.
Wrong move: "30% is bigger than 20%, so II is bigger". That ignores the base (the number the percent is taken of: 150 and 90).
Real values: I = 30, II = 27. So I > II.
Rule: a percentage is only half the story. Always multiply by the actual number.

6Mental-math shortcut: cross-multiply fractions

Which is bigger, 7/9 or 5/7? Multiply across: top of 7/9 times bottom of 5/7 is 7×7 = 49 (this belongs to 7/9). Top of 5/7 times bottom of 7/9 is 5×9 = 45 (this belongs to 5/7). The side with the bigger product is the bigger fraction. So 7/9 > 5/7.

Use it. Equal products mean equal fractions: 6/8 and 9/12 give 6×12 = 72 and 9×8 = 72, so they are equal.

Speed drill

Say which is bigger before you tap Show.

7Lab: fraction comparer

Compares a/b with c/d. Use positive numbers.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "Quantity I: 12.5% of 96. Quantity II: 3/8 of 32. Compare I and II."
  • "Quantity I: x if 3x + 5 = 20. Quantity II: y if 2y − 1 = 11."
  • "Quantity I: x where x² = 49. Quantity II: 5."

Typical answer format for this type: I > II, I < II, I = II, or cannot be determined (sometimes with ≥ and ≤ choices). Read the options first: the exact wording can differ between papers.

Where this type sits. Edutap's topic list includes "Quantity Comparisons" and "Mathematical Inequalities" (a third-party list, not official). Whether this exact format appears in the PFRDA paper is not confirmed by the official notification.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%. Crossing out one option improves the odds.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What are the three steps for comparing? (2) How do I compare two fractions fast? (3) When is the answer "cannot be determined"?
Memorise this
1. Compute I fully, compute II fully, compare.
2. Never judge by percentage alone: multiply by the base.
3. a/b vs c/d: compare a×d with b×c (positive numbers).
4. Two possible values that flip the comparison: cannot be determined.
5. Read the answer options before you start.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Solving Inequalities · Wikipedia: Inequality (mathematics) · Math Is Fun: Inequality symbols · Topic name: Edutap topic list (third-party, not official). Cross-multiplication rule: derived, Python-checked (all a, b, c, d from 1 to 12). Python checks: all values.
UNVERIFIED: the exact option wording used in the real PFRDA paper (shown only as "typical").

Lesson 3: Linking two inequalities (x versus y)

In one line: solve each statement to a boundary, then use the boundaries to link x and y, or admit it cannot be determined.

1Picture this

Asha is taller than 150 cm. Ben is shorter than 150 cm. Who is taller? Asha, without knowing either height. But if Asha is taller than 150 and Ben is ALSO taller than 150, you cannot say.

Ben: below 150Asha: above 150

A common value in the middle is the link between two statements.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Solve each inequality alone first. You get x > something and y < something.
The boundary is the number in each answer (the 4 in x > 4). Put the two boundary numbers side by side.
If x is above a number and y is below the SAME or a lower number, then x is above y. The boundary links them.
If both are above (or both below) the same number, there is no link. Answer: cannot be determined.
Watch equality. x ≥ 3 and y ≤ 3 gives x ≥ y only, because both could be exactly 3.
Two inequalities pointing the same way can be added: if a < b and c < d then a + c < b + d.
Do NOT subtract, multiply or divide two inequalities: it can fail. Subtract: 1 < 2 and 0 < 10, but 1 − 0 = 1 is not below 2 − 10 = −8. Multiply: −3 < −2 and 1 < 2, but −3 × 1 = −3 is not below −2 × 2 = −4. Divide: 1 < 2 and 1 < 10, but 1 ÷ 1 = 1 is not below 2 ÷ 10 = 0.2.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Transitivity (a chain): if a ≤ b and b ≤ c then a ≤ c (strict if either step is strict).
Same-direction inequalities add: a < b and c < d give a + c < b + d. (Reason: a + c < b + c < b + d.)
For positive numbers, 0 < a ≤ b gives 1/a ≥ 1/b: reciprocals reverse. (The reciprocal of a is 1/a. Example: 2 < 5 but 1/2 > 1/5.)
Do not multiply or divide by a variable unless you know its sign.

Transitivity, adding a constant, and positive-number reciprocal rules: Wikipedia "Inequality (mathematics)". Variable-sign caution: Math Is Fun "Solving Inequalities". The add-two-inequalities step is derived from those two rules and checked by Python.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

2x + 1 > 9 and 3y − 2 < 10. Compare x and y.

Step 1. First: subtract 1 from both sides: 2x > 8. Divide by 2: x > 4.
Step 2. Second: add 2 to both sides: 3y < 12. Divide by 3: y < 4.
Step 3. x is above 4 and y is below 4, so x > 4 > y. Answer: x > y.

x > 4 and y > 4. Compare x and y.

Step 1. Try x = 5, y = 6: x < y.
Step 2. Try x = 6, y = 5: x > y.
Step 3. The answer flips. Cannot be determined.

5The trap

Given: x ≥ 3 and y ≤ 3.
Wrong move: "x is at least 3, y is at most 3, so x > y".
Counter-example (one test case that breaks the claim): x = 3 and y = 3. Then x = y, so x > y is false.
Right: x ≥ y. The ≥ must survive to the answer.

Second trap: the reciprocal flip rule is for positive numbers. With mixed signs it fails: −2 < 3 but the reversed rule would predict 1/(−2) > 1/3, but 1/(−2) = −0.5 is smaller than 1/3 = 0.333.

6Mental-math shortcut: boundary check

Solve each side to "x > p" or "y < q". Then compare p and q only. If x is above p, y is below q, and p ≥ q, then x > y. If p < q, you cannot tell.

Use it. x > 6, y < 4: 6 ≥ 4, so x > y. x > 2, y < 5: 2 < 5, so cannot be determined (x = 3, y = 4 vs x = 9, y = 0).

Speed drill

Say the verdict before you tap Show.

7Lab: two boundaries, one verdict

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "I: 2x + 1 > 9. II: 3y − 2 < 10. Compare x and y."
  • "x ≥ 3 and y ≤ 3. Which relation is certainly true?"
  • "If 0 < a < b, which of the following is true?"
Not covered here. Coded or symbolic inequality statements (for example "P > Q = R") are a Reasoning-section topic. This guide does not teach their rule set because it was not sourced.

Wording clue: "certainly true", "must be true" and "definitely" mean the relation holds in EVERY case, so test the edge case x = y.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%. Crossing out one option improves the odds.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) If x is above p and y is below q, when is x > y? (2) What does ≥ plus ≤ at the same number give? (3) Can I add two inequalities?
Memorise this
1. Solve each statement to x > p, y < q first.
2. p ≥ q: x > y. Otherwise: cannot be determined.
3. Both above (or both below) one number: no link.
4. x ≥ 3 and y ≤ 3 gives x ≥ y, not x > y.
5. Same-direction inequalities add. Do not subtract or multiply them.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Solving Inequalities · Wikipedia: Inequality (mathematics) · Math Is Fun: Inequality symbols · Adding two same-direction inequalities: derived, Python-checked (20,000 random integer cases). Python checks: boundary logic and counter-examples.
UNVERIFIED: coded/symbolic inequalities (Reasoning topic, no source fetched); number-line open/closed-circle conventions and compound a < x < b notation (the fetched pages did not cover them, so omitted).

Time and Work, Pipes and Cisterns

Two lessons. Pipes are the same maths as work: a pipe is just a worker who fills a tank. Do lesson 1, then stop. Lesson 2 later.

Lesson 1: Time and Work

In one line: if someone finishes a job in 10 days, they do 1/10 of it per day, and workers together simply add their per-day shares.

1Picture this

Two painters are hired for a flat in Jaipur. Raju alone paints it in 10 days. Meena alone takes 15 days. Both start on Monday morning. Who finishes the flat faster than either one alone? Both of them together, obviously. But how many days?

Raju: 3 patches a dayMeena: 2

Cut the flat into 30 equal patches. Raju does 3 a day, Meena 2 a day. Together 5 a day, so 30 ÷ 5 = 6 days.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Call the whole job "1 job". It could be a wall, a report, anything.
If you finish in 10 days, then in 1 day you do 1/10 of the job. That per-day share is your rate.
Slow worker = small share per day. Fast worker = big share per day.
Workers at the same time: add their per-day shares. That is the combined rate.
Days needed = 1 job ÷ combined rate. Fractions are painful, so pick a friendly total number of "units" for the job.
LCM trick: choose the total units as the LCM of the days (the smallest number both divide into; see chapter 10). Then each worker's daily units are whole numbers.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Work = Rate × Time. One worker's rate = 1 ÷ (days alone).
Together: 1/a + 1/b = 1/c, where a, b are the days alone and c is the days together.
Two workers shortcut: c = (a × b) ÷ (a + b).
LCM method: total units = LCM of the days. Daily units = total ÷ days. Days together = total ÷ (sum of daily units).

Rate = 1/time, work = rate × time, rates add, and the 1/c = 1/a + 1/b form: Focus Tutoring "Combined Work Problems"; (a×b)÷(a+b) form: QuickMath "Combined work rate problems"; LCM definition: Wikipedia "Least common multiple". The LCM-as-total-units trick is just choosing a convenient unit, so the result is unchanged.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

A does a job in 10 days, B in 15 days. Together?

Step 1. LCM of 10 and 15 = 30. Say the job = 30 units.
Step 2. A: 30 ÷ 10 = 3 units a day. B: 30 ÷ 15 = 2 units a day.
Step 3. Together 3 + 2 = 5 units a day.
Step 4. 30 ÷ 5 = 6 days. Check with the shortcut: 10 × 15 = 150, 10 + 15 = 25, 150 ÷ 25 = 6.

A and B together take 12 days. A alone takes 20 days. How long does B alone take?

Step 1. LCM of 12 and 20 = 60 units.
Step 2. Together: 60 ÷ 12 = 5 a day. A alone: 60 ÷ 20 = 3 a day.
Step 3. B = 5 − 3 = 2 a day (subtract to find the missing worker).
Step 4. 60 ÷ 2 = 30 days.

A takes 12 days, B takes 18 days. They work on alternate days, A first. When is the job done?

Step 1. LCM of 12 and 18 = 36 units. A: 36 ÷ 12 = 3 a day. B: 36 ÷ 18 = 2 a day.
Step 2. A two-day cycle (A then B) = 5 units.
Step 3. 7 cycles = 14 days = 7 × 5 = 35 units (8 cycles would be 40, more than 36). 36 − 35 = 1 unit is left.
Step 4. Day 15 is A's turn. A does 3 units a day, so 1 unit needs 1/3 of a day.
Answer: 14 1/3 days.

A is twice as fast as B. Together they take 12 days. A alone?

Step 1. Twice as fast: A does 2 units a day for every 1 unit of B. Together 2 + 1 = 3 units a day.
Step 2. In 12 days the job is 12 × 3 = 36 units.
Step 3. A alone: 36 ÷ 2 = 18 days. (B alone: 36 ÷ 1 = 36 days.)

Wages. A (10 days) and B (15 days) finish a job and are paid ₹1,500 in proportion to the work each did. With 30 units, A did 3 per day and B 2 per day over the same days, so the work ratio is 3 : 2. The 5 is 3 + 2. A gets 1,500 × 3/5 = ₹900, B gets 1,500 × 2/5 = ₹600. The question has to say the pay follows work done; do not assume it.

5The trap

A takes 10 days, B takes 15 days. Together?
Wrong move: "average the days": (10 + 15) ÷ 2 = 12.5 days. Together can never be slower than the faster person alone (10 days)!
Right: 6 days. Rule of thumb: the answer must be smaller than the smaller time. If it is not, you have made an error.

6Mental-math shortcut: pick the total, split, add

Two habits. (1) Pick the LCM as total units. (2) For two workers say "product over sum": 10 × 15 ÷ 25 = 6. Start with small pairs where the numbers divide neatly.

A aloneB aloneLCM (units)Daily unitsTogether
612122 + 1 = 34 days
1015303 + 2 = 56 days
1224242 + 1 = 38 days
2030603 + 2 = 512 days
Two same-speed workers halve the time: 8 and 8 gives 4 days. If B takes exactly twice as long as A, together = two thirds of A's time (12 and 24 gives 8).

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show. Use the LCM.

7Lab: work calculator

Days alone for each worker. Put 0 in C if there is no third worker.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A can do a work in 15 days and B in 20 days. They work together for 4 days. What fraction of work is left?"
  • "A and B together finish in 12 days. A alone in 20 days. Find B alone."
  • "A is twice as efficient as B. Together they take 12 days. How long would A take alone?"
  • "A, B, C are paid in proportion to work done. Find B's share of ₹2,700."
  • Time and Work: 1 question in 2025 and 1 question in 2022, reported in memory-based exam analyses (edutap). So expect about one, not a block.

Word clues: "twice as efficient" means rate ratio 2 : 1, so time ratio 1 : 2. "Alternate days" means a repeating two-day cycle. "Leaves after" means that worker stops adding units.

Typical wrong options (typical-style, not sourced to a paper). Watch for the average of the days, the sum of the days, and the answer when the second worker is ignored. Remember: together must be smaller than the smaller time.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%: 0.3125 ÷ (1.25 + 0.3125) = 0.2.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What is A's rate if A takes 10 days? (2) Which total do I pick? (3) How do I find a missing worker? (4) Why can the average of the days never be right?
Memorise this
1. Rate = 1 ÷ days. Work = Rate × Time.
2. Total units = LCM of the days.
3. Add daily units to work together; subtract to find a missing worker.
4. Two workers: (a × b) ÷ (a + b).
5. Together is always less than the smaller time.

Sources: Focus Tutoring: combined work problems · QuickMath: combined work rate · Wikipedia: Least common multiple · Exam facts: PFRDA 2026 notification. Past counts: edutap exam analyses (memory-based). All worked numbers checked by Python. Derived, Python-checked: wage split (only when the question states pay follows work done, it is then plain ratio arithmetic), "twice as efficient" as a 2 : 1 rate ratio (from rate = 1 ÷ time), and "together is less than the smaller time".
UNVERIFIED: none.

Lesson 2: Pipes and Cisterns

In one line: a pipe is a worker; an inlet (a pipe that fills a tank) has a positive rate, and an outlet or leak (drains it) has a negative rate. A cistern is just a tank.

1Picture this

The overhead tank on your terrace in Patna. The motor pipe fills it in 12 hours. A tap at the bottom drains it in 20 hours. Leave both on. Does the tank fill? Yes, but slowly, because the tap steals part of the water.

Fills 5 an hourDrains 3

Tank = 60 units. In goes 5 an hour, out goes 3 an hour, net 2. 60 ÷ 2 = 30 hours.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

An inlet pipe fills the tank. An outlet pipe, or a leak, empties it.
A pipe that fills the tank in 12 hours fills 1/12 of it each hour. Same as work.
An outlet that empties it in 20 hours removes 1/20 each hour.
Pipes open together: add the inlets, subtract the outlets. That is the net rate.
Pick the LCM of all the times as the tank size in "litres". Then each pipe's litres per hour are whole numbers.
Time = tank ÷ net litres per hour. If the net is zero or negative, the tank never fills.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Inlet rate = +1/n per hour. Outlet or leak rate = −1/n per hour.
Net rate = (sum of inlet rates) − (sum of outlet rates).
Time to fill = tank ÷ net rate (only if net is positive).
Leak: a pipe fills in x hours, but with the leak open it takes y hours (y > x). The leak alone empties the full tank in 1 ÷ (1/x − 1/y) hours.

Inlet/outlet definitions, 1/n per hour, net work = inlets − outlets: GeeksforGeeks "Pipes and Cistern". GeeksforGeeks gives the net hourly fill (y − x)/(xy) for a filler (x h) with an emptier (y h). The leak-alone formula 1 ÷ (1/x − 1/y) = xy ÷ (y − x) is derived, Python-checked from the net-rate rule.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Pipes A (12 hours) and B (15 hours) fill a tank. Pipe C empties it in 20 hours. All three open. Time to fill?

Step 1. LCM of 12, 15, 20 = 60 litres.
Step 2. A: 60 ÷ 12 = +5 an hour. B: 60 ÷ 15 = +4 an hour. C: 60 ÷ 20 = −3 an hour (minus because it empties).
Step 3. Net = 5 + 4 − 3 = 6 litres an hour.
Step 4. 60 ÷ 6 = 10 hours.

A pipe fills a tank in 6 hours. With a leak it takes 8 hours. How long does the leak alone take to empty the full tank?

Step 1. LCM of 6 and 8 = 24 litres.
Step 2. Pipe alone: 24 ÷ 6 = 4 an hour. With leak: 24 ÷ 8 = 3 an hour.
Step 3. The leak takes away 4 − 3 = 1 litre an hour.
Step 4. 24 ÷ 1 = 24 hours.

Pipes A (10 hours) and B (15 hours) both open for 3 hours, then A is closed. Total time to fill?

Step 1. LCM of 10 and 15 = 30 litres. A: 30 ÷ 10 = 3 an hour. B: 30 ÷ 15 = 2 an hour.
Step 2. First 3 hours: both open, 3 + 2 = 5 an hour, so 5 × 3 = 15 litres. Half the tank.
Step 3. Left: 15 litres. B alone does 2 an hour: 15 ÷ 2 = 7.5 hours.
Step 4. Total = 3 + 7.5 = 10.5 hours.

5The trap

Inlets A (12 h), B (15 h), outlet C (20 h), all open.
Wrong move: add all three rates as if C also fills: 1/12 + 1/15 + 1/20 = 1/5, so 5 hours.
Right: subtract the outlet. 1/12 + 1/15 − 1/20 = 1/10, so 10 hours. Also never write "leak time = 8 − 6 = 2 hours": rates subtract, times do not.

6Mental-math shortcut: litres per hour, then net

Choose the LCM, convert each pipe to litres per hour, then add or subtract small whole numbers. You never touch a fraction.

InletOutletLitresNet per hourTime
12 h20 h605 − 3 = 230 h
4 h6 h123 − 2 = 112 h
8 h24 h243 − 1 = 212 h
Sanity check. With an outlet open, the time must be longer than the inlet alone. If you get shorter, you added the outlet.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: pipes calculator

Hours to fill (or empty) the whole tank alone. Put 0 in B or C if there is no such pipe.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "Pipe A fills a tank in 10 hours and B in 15 hours. If both are opened together, how long to fill?"
  • "Two pipes fill a tank in 12 and 15 hours. A third empties it in 20 hours. All open together. Time?"
  • "A tank fills in 6 hours, but a leak makes it 8 hours. How long does the leak take to empty the full tank?"
  • "Both pipes are opened, and after some hours one is closed. Total time?"
  • Pipes and Cisterns appears in a third-party (Oliveboard) topic list under miscellaneous arithmetic. It is not official. For 2022 and 2025, Time and Work is 1 question each and pipes is not listed separately, reported in memory-based exam analyses (edutap). Treat pipes as a bonus on top of the work method.

Word clues: "fills" means plus, "empties" or "leak" means minus, "closed after" means that pipe stops adding.

Typical wrong options (typical-style, not sourced to a paper). Watch for the answer from adding the outlet, the answer from ignoring the outlet, and the difference of times. Check the direction: outlet open means a longer time.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What sign does an outlet get? (2) Which total do I pick? (3) What does zero net rate mean? (4) With an outlet open, is the fill time shorter or longer than the inlet alone?
Memorise this
1. Inlet +1/n, outlet or leak −1/n per hour.
2. Tank = LCM of all times.
3. Net = inlets − outlets, in litres per hour.
4. Time = tank ÷ net. Net 0 or less: never fills.
5. Rates add and subtract; times never do.

Sources: GeeksforGeeks: Pipes and Cistern · Focus Tutoring: combined work problems · Wikipedia: Least common multiple · Third-party topic list (not official): Oliveboard. All worked numbers checked by Python. Leak-alone formula: derived, Python-checked.
UNVERIFIED: that pipes questions appear in the actual paper.

Time, Speed, Distance, Trains, Boats

Three lessons. Do one per sitting: (1) speed basics and average speed, (2) relative speed and trains, (3) boats and streams. Reported in memory-based exam analyses: TSD 2 questions in 2022, Trains 1 question in 2025. Third-party topic lists (not official) include Boats and Streams. Races are left out because no page was fetched for them.

Lesson 1 · Speed, units, average speed

In one line: speed is distance covered in one unit of time, and the average speed of a trip is total distance divided by total time.

1Picture this

You drive from Jaipur towards Delhi. After 2 hours your trip meter shows 120 km. In every single hour you covered 60 km on average. That "60 km in 1 hour" is the speed: 60 km/h.

Hour 1: 60 kmHour 2: 60 km

Three numbers always travel together: distance, speed, time. Know any two and you can get the third.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Speed tells you how much distance is covered in 1 unit of time.
60 km/h means 60 km each hour. So in 3 hours you cover 3 × 60 = 180 km.
Need time instead? Distance ÷ speed. 180 km at 60 km/h takes 180 ÷ 60 = 3 hours.
Units must match. km with hours, metres with seconds. Mixed units are the number one source of wrong answers. km/h means kilometres per hour; m/s means metres per second.
Minutes to hours: divide by 60. 45 minutes = 45 ÷ 60 = 0.75 hours.
Why 5/18? 1 km = 1000 m and 1 hour = 3600 s, so 1 km/h = 1000/3600 m/s = 5/18 m/s.
Average speed of a whole trip = total distance ÷ total time. Not the average of the two speeds.
If you ride the same distance each way at two speeds, the slow leg takes more time, so it counts more. The answer lands closer to the slow speed.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Speed = Distance ÷ Time  ·  Distance = Speed × Time  ·  Time = Distance ÷ Speed
Average speed = Total distance ÷ Total time
km/h to m/s: multiply by 5/18 (same as ÷ 3.6). m/s to km/h: multiply by 18/5 (× 3.6).
Same distance each way at speeds a and b (just the two speeds): average = 2ab ÷ (a + b)

Speed and average speed: Wikipedia "Speed". 3.6 km/h = 1 m/s: Wikipedia "Kilometres per hour". The 2ab/(a+b) form is the harmonic mean for equal distances: Wikipedia "Harmonic mean".

4Worked examples, one tap per step

A bus covers 150 km in 2.5 hours. Speed?

Step 1. Distance = 150 km, time = 2.5 h.
Step 2. Speed = 150 ÷ 2.5.
Step 3. Double both numbers to remove the decimal: 150 ÷ 2.5 = 300 ÷ 5 = 60. Answer: 60 km/h.

Go to a town at 60 km/h and return the same road at 40 km/h. Average speed for the round trip?

Step 1. Pick an easy distance each way. Say 120 km (the answer does not depend on it).
Step 2. Time going = 120 ÷ 60 = 2 h. Time returning = 120 ÷ 40 = 3 h. Total time = 5 h.
Step 3. Total distance = 240 km. Average = 240 ÷ 5 = 48 km/h.
Check with the rule: 2 × 60 × 40 ÷ (60 + 40) = 4800 ÷ 100 = 48.

5The trap

Same question: 60 km/h out, 40 km/h back.
Wrong move: (60 + 40) ÷ 2 = 50 km/h. This assumes equal TIME at each speed.
But you spent 3 hours at 40 and only 2 hours at 60. So the answer must be below 50. Right: 48 km/h.

6Mental-math: km/h to m/s

Multiply by 5/18. Easy when the km/h number is a multiple of 18. Learn these six first.

km/hm/skm/hm/s
1857220
36109025
541510830

Each pair is km/h × 5/18 = m/s. Going the other way, multiply by 18/5 (or: each 1 m/s is 3.6 km/h).

Use it. For 45 km/h: 45 ÷ 18 = 2.5, times 5 = 12.5 m/s. Divide by 18 first, then multiply by 5.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: speed calculator and average speed

Round trip, same distance each way

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A car covers the first half of a journey at 40 km/h and the second half at 60 km/h. Find the average speed."
  • "A man travels for 2 hours at 50 km/h and 3 hours at 60 km/h. Average speed for the whole journey?"
  • "Convert 72 km/h to m/s." or "A cyclist covers 200 m in 20 s. Speed in km/h?"
  • "If he walks at 5 km/h instead of 4 km/h, he reaches 15 minutes earlier. Find the distance." (typical style; a harder type this guide does not teach, skip it on a first pass)

Reported in memory-based exam analyses: TSD 2 questions in 2022 (edutap). Word clues: "half the journey" means equal distance. "for 2 hours then 3 hours" means different times.

How the wrong options are built. The plain mean of the two speeds is almost always planted. Also: forgetting to change minutes into hours, and mixing km/h with m/s.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20% (derived, Python-checked: 1.25p − 0.3125(1 − p) > 0 when p > 0.2).

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Three forms of the speed formula? (2) How do I turn km/h into m/s? (3) Is average speed the mean of the speeds? (4) What is the formula for equal distances?
Memorise this
1. S = D ÷ T, D = S × T, T = D ÷ S.
2. km/h × 5/18 = m/s. m/s × 18/5 = km/h.
3. Average speed = total distance ÷ total time.
4. Equal distances: 2ab ÷ (a + b).
5. Change minutes to hours first (45 min = 0.75 h).

Sources: Wikipedia: Speed · Wikipedia: Kilometres per hour · Wikipedia: Harmonic mean. 2022 TSD count: edutap exam analysis (memory-based). All worked numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: none for this lesson's maths. The "5/18" form is the 3.6 conversion rewritten (3.6 = 18/5).

Lesson 2 · Relative speed and trains

In one line: when two things move, work with the gap between them: same direction, speeds subtract; opposite directions, speeds add.

1Picture this

You sit in a train doing 60 km/h. A second train runs on the next track in the SAME direction at 50 km/h. For you it creeps past slowly, at only 10 km/h. Now it comes the OPPOSITE way at 50 km/h. It whooshes past at 110 km/h.

10Opposite: 60 + 50 = 110

Same direction: 60 − 50 = 10. Opposite: 60 + 50 = 110.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Relative speed is how fast one object moves as seen from the other.
Same direction on a line: the faster one pulls away by the DIFFERENCE of speeds.
Opposite directions: the gap changes by the SUM of speeds.
A train passing a pole (or a standing man) must cover its OWN length. The pole has no length.
A train crossing a platform must cover its own length PLUS the platform length. Front reaches the platform start, back leaves the platform end.
Two trains crossing: add both lengths, and use relative speed. Then time = distance ÷ speed, in matching units (metres with m/s).

3The rules (now that you get it)

Same direction: relative speed = bigger − smaller. Opposite: relative speed = sum.
Train crosses pole: distance = train length. Crosses platform: train + platform.
Two trains crossing: distance = sum of lengths. Time = (L1 + L2) ÷ relative speed.

Relative velocity in 1D, vB|A = vB − vA (with opposite directions the signs make this a sum): Wikipedia "Relative velocity". Train length rules: derived, Python-checked (from distance = speed × time).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

A 180 m train runs at 72 km/h. Time to pass a pole? Time to cross a 270 m platform?

Step 1. 72 km/h = 72 × 5/18. 72 ÷ 18 = 4, then 4 × 5 = 20 m/s.
Step 2. Pole: distance 180 m. Time = 180 ÷ 20 = 9 s.
Step 3. Platform: distance = 180 + 270 = 450 m. Time = 450 ÷ 20 = 22.5 s.

Trains of 120 m and 130 m run at 60 km/h and 40 km/h. Time to cross each other if (a) opposite, (b) same direction?

Step 1. Distance to clear = 120 + 130 = 250 m.
Step 2 (opposite). Relative speed = 60 + 40 = 100 km/h = 100 × 5/18 = 500/18 = 250/9 m/s.
Step 3. Time = 250 ÷ (250/9). Dividing by a fraction means multiplying by its flip: 250 × 9/250 = 9 s.
Step 4 (same direction). Relative speed = 60 − 40 = 20 km/h = 20 × 5/18 = 100/18 = 50/9 m/s. Time = 250 × 9/50 = 2250/50 = 45 s.

5The trap

180 m train at 72 km/h crosses a pole.
Wrong move 1: 180 ÷ 72 = 2.5 s. Metres were divided by km/h. Convert to 20 m/s first: 9 s.
Wrong move 2 (platform of 270 m): 270 ÷ 20 = 13.5 s. Left out the train's own length. Right: 450 ÷ 20 = 22.5 s.

6Mental-math: sum or difference, then convert

Two quick steps every time: (1) sum or difference of km/h, (2) times 5/18 if lengths are in metres.

Relative (km/h)m/s
185
3610
5415
7220
9025
Use it. Opposite, 50 and 40 km/h: sum 90 km/h = 25 m/s. Lengths 100 m and 150 m: 250 ÷ 25 = 10 s.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: train crossing calculator

Two trains crossing

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A train 150 m long running at 54 km/h passes a pole. Find the time taken."
  • "A 200 m train crosses a 300 m platform in how many seconds if its speed is 36 km/h?"
  • "Two trains of lengths 100 m and 140 m run in opposite directions at 50 and 70 km/h. In how much time do they cross each other?"
  • "A train crosses a pole in 12 s and is 240 m long. Find its speed in km/h."

Reported in memory-based exam analyses: Trains 1 question in 2025 (edutap). Word clues: "passes a pole / man" means own length only. "crosses a platform / bridge" means add its length. "overtakes" or "same direction" means subtract speeds.

How the wrong options are built. Expect the answer for train length only, the answer for platform length only, the answer with speeds subtracted instead of added, and the answer with units not converted.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20% (derived, Python-checked: 1.25p − 0.3125(1 − p) > 0 when p > 0.2).

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Same direction: sum or difference? (2) What distance for a pole? For a platform? (3) What distance when two trains cross? (4) Which unit pair must match?
Memorise this
1. Same direction: subtract. Opposite: add.
2. Pole: own length. Platform: own + platform.
3. Two trains: add both lengths.
4. Metres go with m/s: km/h × 5/18.
5. Time = distance ÷ relative speed.

Sources: Wikipedia: Relative velocity · Wikipedia: Kilometres per hour. 2025 Trains count: edutap exam analysis (memory-based). All worked numbers checked by Python. Pole/platform/two-train length rules: derived, Python-checked.
UNVERIFIED: none.

Lesson 3 · Boats and streams

In one line: a river adds its speed to a boat going with it and takes it away from a boat going against it.

1Picture this

A boatman on the Ganga at Varanasi rows at 6 km/h in still water. Going with the current, which flows at 2 km/h, the bank slips past at 8 km/h. Coming back against the current, only 4 km/h.

Downstream 6 + 2 = 8Up 4

Same boatman, same effort. The stream decides the difference.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Boat speed means the speed in still water, written b. Stream speed is the speed of the water, written s.
Downstream (with the current): the speeds add. Speed over the bank = b + s.
Upstream (against the current): the stream pulls back. Speed over the bank = b − s.
This is the same idea as relative speed in Lesson 2: the water carries the boat like a moving train carries a walking man.
Everything else is distance ÷ speed, using the downstream or upstream speed for that leg.
Given the two speeds, work backwards: add them and halve to get b; subtract and halve to get s.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Downstream D = b + s  ·  Upstream U = b − s
Still water b = (D + U) ÷ 2  ·  Stream s = (D − U) ÷ 2
Time on a leg = distance ÷ (D or U, whichever matches that leg)

Adding velocities when one object moves on a moving medium: Wikipedia "Relative velocity" (man walking on a moving train, 10 + 40 = 50 km/h). The inverse formulas follow by adding and subtracting the two equations; checked by Python over many (b, s) pairs.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

A boat does 12 km/h in still water; the stream flows at 3 km/h. How long for 45 km downstream and back?

Step 1. Downstream = 12 + 3 = 15 km/h. Upstream = 12 − 3 = 9 km/h.
Step 2. Time down = 45 ÷ 15 = 3 h.
Step 3. Time up = 45 ÷ 9 = 5 h.
Step 4. Total = 3 + 5 = 8 hours. (Round-trip average speed = total distance 45 + 45 = 90 km, then 90 ÷ 8 = 11.25 km/h, below 12.)

A boat goes downstream at 20 km/h and upstream at 12 km/h. Find boat and stream speed.

Step 1. b = (20 + 12) ÷ 2 = 32 ÷ 2 = 16 km/h.
Step 2. s = (20 − 12) ÷ 2 = 8 ÷ 2 = 4 km/h.
Check: 16 + 4 = 20 and 16 − 4 = 12.

5The trap

Boat 12 km/h, stream 3 km/h, 45 km each way (same example).
Wrong move: "the stream helps going and hurts coming back, so it cancels". Time = 90 ÷ 12 = 7.5 h.
But the slow upstream leg takes longer than the fast leg saves. Right: 3 + 5 = 8 h. A stream always makes the round trip slower.

6Mental-math: the sum-and-half habit

Pick the two given speeds; the unknown is always one step away.

bsDownUp
102128
123159
82106
1642012
Use it. Down 15, up 9: b = 24 ÷ 2 = 12, s = 6 ÷ 2 = 3. Add, halve. Subtract, halve.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: boat and stream calculator

Work backwards

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A boat's speed in still water is 10 km/h and the stream flows at 2 km/h. What is the downstream speed?"
  • "A man rows downstream at 18 km/h and upstream at 10 km/h. Find the speed of the stream."
  • "A boat takes 4 hours to cover 24 km upstream. If the boat speed is 8 km/h, what is the stream speed?" (typical style)
  • "A boat goes 18 km up and 18 km down in how many hours, if it does 6 km/h in still water and the stream is 3 km/h?"

Boats and Streams appears in a third-party topic list (Oliveboard, not official). It was not named in the 2022 or 2025 counts we have, so treat it as lower priority than Lesson 1 and 2.

How the wrong options are built. Expect: the sum instead of the difference, the still-water speed in place of the stream speed, and the time for only one leg.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20% (derived, Python-checked: 1.25p − 0.3125(1 − p) > 0 when p > 0.2).

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Downstream speed formula? (2) Upstream? (3) How do I get still-water speed from the two? (4) And the stream?
Memorise this
1. Down = b + s. Up = b − s.
2. b = (D + U) ÷ 2.
3. s = (D − U) ÷ 2.
4. Use the matching speed for each leg.
5. A stream always slows a round trip versus still water.

Sources: Wikipedia: Relative velocity. Topic list: Oliveboard (third-party, not official). b = (D + U) ÷ 2, s = (D − U) ÷ 2 and "a stream always slows a round trip" (2db/(b² − s²) > 2d/b for s > 0): derived, Python-checked. All worked numbers checked by Python.

Lesson 1 · Area and perimeter (flat shapes)

In one line: perimeter is the walk around the edge, area is the number of unit squares inside, and every flat-shape formula is one of five you can memorise.

1Picture this

A rectangular plot in a village is 12 m long and 8 m wide. The farmer wants a fence round it (that is the perimeter) and wants to lay tiles on the floor of a hut built on it (that is the area).

Area 96 m² (tiles)Fence 40 m

Area: 12 × 8 = 96 square metres. Fence: 12 + 8 + 12 + 8 = 40 metres. Different units, different questions.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Perimeter = total length of the boundary. Its unit is a length (m, cm).
Area = how many 1-by-1 squares fit inside. Its unit is square (m², cm²).
Rectangle: rows × columns of squares, so area = length × width.
A triangle is half of a rectangle with the same base and height, so area = half × base × height. (Base = the side it sits on. Height = the straight-up distance from the base to the opposite corner.)
A trapezium has two parallel sides (they never meet, like railway lines). Average those two sides, then multiply by the height between them.
A diagonal is the straight line from one corner to the opposite corner. The sign √ means square root: the number that, multiplied by itself, gives the number inside. √289 = 17 because 17 × 17 = 289. Also a² means a × a (so 5² = 25).
Percent change of a side: +10% means × 1.10, −10% means × 0.90 (percent factors, chapter 04). Area uses two sides, so multiply two factors: 1.10 × 1.10 = 1.21, a 21% rise.
A circle uses the number π (about 3.14159). Many questions say "take π = 22/7".

3The rules (now that you get it)

Rectangle (sides l, w): Area = l × w · Perimeter = 2(l + w) · Diagonal = √(l² + w²)
Square (side a): Area = a² · Perimeter = 4a · Diagonal = a√2
Triangle: Area = ½ × base × height (height is at right angles to the base)
Circle (radius r): Circumference = 2πr · Area = πr²
Trapezium (parallel sides a, b; height h): Area = ½(a + b) × h

Formulas as stated on the Math Is Fun pages listed in Sources. a√2 means a × √2, about 1.41 × a.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Rectangle 15 m by 8 m. Find area, perimeter and diagonal.

Step 1. Area = 15 × 8 = 120 m².
Step 2. Perimeter = 2 × (15 + 8) = 2 × 23 = 46 m.
Step 3. Diagonal = √(15² + 8²) = √(225 + 64) = √289.
Step 4. 17 × 17 = 289, so diagonal = 17 m.

Circle of radius 7 cm, π = 22/7. Find area and circumference.

Step 1. Area = πr² = 22/7 × 7 × 7.
Step 2. The 7 on the bottom cancels one of the 7s on top, leaving 22 × 7 = 154 cm².
Step 3. Circumference = 2πr = 2 × 22/7 × 7 = 2 × 22 = 44 cm.

Trapezium: parallel sides 10 and 14, height 6. Area?

Step 1. Average of parallel sides = (10 + 14) ÷ 2 = 12.
Step 2. Area = 12 × 6 = 72 square units.

5The trap

A square has side 5, so area 25. The side is doubled to 10.
Wrong move: "side doubled, so area doubled to 50".
Right: new area = 10 × 10 = 100, which is 4 times 25. Area scales with the SQUARE of the length. The same holds for a circle: double the radius, area becomes 4 times.

6Mental-math shortcut: π = 22/7 with multiples of 7

22/7 is an approximation of π (the real value is 3.14159...). When the radius is a multiple of 7, the 7 cancels and there is no hard division. Learn this small table.

RadiusCircumference 2πrArea πr²
744154
1488616
211321386
281762464
Use it. Radius 14: circumference is double that of 7 (44 to 88), but area is 4 times (154 to 616). The pattern is the trap from card 5.
Where the table comes from. Area: r = 14 gives 22 × 2 × 14 = 616; r = 21 gives 22 × 3 × 21 = 1386; r = 28 gives 22 × 4 × 28 = 2464 (the 7 cancels, leaving r ÷ 7 as a small number). Circumference: 2 × 22 × (r ÷ 7), so 88, 132, 176.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show. Take π = 22/7 where needed.

7Lab: shape calculator

Rectangle
Circle

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "The length of a rectangle is increased by 20% and the breadth decreased by 10%. Find the change in area." (Percent factors, chapter 04: 1.20 × 0.90 = 1.08, so +8%.)
  • "The perimeter of a square equals the circumference of a circle of radius 14. Find the side." (Circumference 2 × 22/7 × 14 = 88, side 88 ÷ 4 = 22.)
  • "A rectangular park is 40 m by 25 m. A path 2 m wide runs inside along the boundary. Find the area of the path." (Big: 40 × 25 = 1000. Inner rectangle loses 2 m on each side, so 36 × 21 = 756. Path = 1000 − 756 = 244 m².)
  • Wording clues: "take π = 22/7" tells you to cancel with a multiple of 7; "area of the path" means big area minus small area.

A rectangle question was reported as 1 of 20 in 2025 in memory-based exam analyses (edutap). Mensuration also appears in a third-party topic list (Oliveboard, not official).

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance is above 20%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Rectangle area and perimeter? (2) Triangle area? (3) Circle area and circumference? (4) What happens to area if the side doubles?
Memorise this
1. Rectangle: l×w and 2(l+w).
2. Triangle: ½ × base × height.
3. Circle: 2πr and πr² (π = 22/7 with multiples of 7).
4. Trapezium: ½(a+b)×h.
5. Length ×2 means area ×4.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Area · Rectangle · Square · Circle · Trapezoid · Wikipedia: Pi (22/7 as a common approximation). Area-scaling rule (length ×2 gives area ×4) and percentage-change factors (1.2 × 0.9, 1.1²): derived, Python-checked. 2025 rectangle count: edutap exam analysis (memory-based). Lesson 2 · Volume and surface area (solids)

In one line: volume is how much a solid holds, surface area is how much paint covers its outside, and cone and sphere formulas hang off the cylinder.

1Picture this

A carton (a box shape, called a cuboid) of tea packets measures 10 cm by 4 cm by 5 cm. How many 1 cm cubes fit inside? That count is the volume. How much brown paper wraps it with no overlap? That is the surface area.

Volume 200 cm³Paper 220 cm²

Volume = 10 × 4 × 5 = 200. Paper = six faces: 2 × (40 + 50 + 20) = 220.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Volume unit is cubic (cm³, m³). Surface area unit is square (cm², m²).
Names: cuboid = a box. Cube = a box with all edges equal. Cylinder = a tin can. Cone = an ice-cream cone. Sphere = a ball. a³ means a × a × a.
A cuboid is layers of a rectangle: base area × height.
A cylinder is layers of a circle: base area × height = πr² × h.
A cone with the same base and height as a cylinder holds exactly one third as much.
Surface area = add up every face. A cube has 6 equal square faces, so 6a². A cylinder: two circles (2πr²) plus one curved sheet that unrolls into a rectangle (2πrh), total 2πr(r + h).
A cone's curved side uses the slant height s (along the surface), not the straight height h. Cone total = base πr² + curved πrs = πr(r + s).
Space diagonal = corner to the opposite corner through the inside of the box. It is Pythagoras twice (the right-triangle rule is taught in Lesson 3).
Scaling: if EVERY length is doubled, area is ×4 and volume ×8. If only a cylinder's radius is doubled (height unchanged), V = πr²h gets ×4 because r is squared. If a solid is melted and recast, its volume stays the same.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Cuboid (l, w, h): V = lwh · Surface = 2(lw + lh + wh) · Space diagonal = √(l² + w² + h²)
Cube (edge a): V = a³ · Surface = 6a² · Space diagonal = a√3
Cylinder: V = πr²h · Curved surface = 2πrh · Total = 2πr(r + h)
Cone: slant s = √(r² + h²) · V = ⅓πr²h · Curved = πrs · Total = πr(r + s)
Sphere: V = (4/3)πr³ · Surface = 4πr²

Formulas as stated on the pages listed in Sources.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Cylinder, r = 7, h = 10, π = 22/7. Volume, curved and total surface?

Step 1. Volume = 22/7 × 7 × 7 × 10 = 22 × 7 × 10 = 1540.
Step 2. Curved = 2πrh = 2 × 22/7 × 7 × 10 = 44 × 10 = 440.
Step 3. Total = 2πr(r + h) = 44 × (7 + 10) = 44 × 17 = 748.

Cone, r = 7, h = 24, π = 22/7. Slant height, volume, curved surface?

Step 1. s = √(7² + 24²) = √(49 + 576) = √625 = 25.
Step 2. Volume = ⅓ × 22/7 × 49 × 24. Cancel: 24 ÷ 3 = 8 and 49 ÷ 7 = 7. So 22 × 7 × 8 = 1232.
Step 3. Curved = πrs = 22/7 × 7 × 25 = 550.

Sphere, r = 21, π = 22/7. Volume and surface?

Step 1. Volume = 4/3 × 22/7 × 21³ = 4/3 × 22/7 × 9261.
Step 2. 9261 ÷ 7 = 1323 and 1323 ÷ 3 = 441, so volume = 4 × 22 × 441 = 38808.
Step 3. Surface = 4 × 22/7 × 21². Here 21² = 441 and 441 ÷ 7 = 63, so 4 × 22 × 63 = 5544.

5The trap

A cube has edge 3, volume 27. The edge is doubled to 6.
Wrong move: "doubled edge, doubled volume" (54), or copying the area rule and saying 4 times (108).
Right: 6 × 6 × 6 = 216, which is 8 times 27. Volume scales with the CUBE of length.
Second trap: in a cone's volume use the straight height h, never the slant height s. For r = 3, h = 4, s = 5: right is ⅓π·9·4 = 12π, using s gives 15π (wrong).

6Mental-math shortcut: cubes table and the 3 : 1 : 2 rule

Edge aa³ (volume)6a² (surface)

5125150

3 : 1 : 2 rule. If a cylinder, a cone and a sphere share radius r and the height is 2r, their volumes are in the ratio 3 : 1 : 2 (cylinder : cone : sphere). Check from the formulas: cylinder 2πr³, cone ⅔πr³, sphere (4/3)πr³. Also: same base and height, cone = one third of cylinder.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show. Take π = 22/7.

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A cuboid of 10 cm × 4 cm × 5 cm: find its total surface area."
  • "The radius of a cylinder is doubled and the height is unchanged. Find the change in volume."
  • "A solid metal cube is melted and recast into smaller cubes or a cylinder. How many pieces?" (Idea: volume stays the same, so equate the two volumes.)
  • "Find the cost of painting the curved surface at ₹ x per square metre." (Area × rate.)

Memorise this
1. Cuboid: lwh and 2(lw+lh+wh). Cube: a³ and 6a².
2. Cylinder: πr²h and 2πr(r+h).

4. Sphere: 4/3 πr³ and 4πr².
5. Length ×2: area ×4, volume ×8.

Sources: Wikipedia: Rectangular cuboid · Wikipedia: Cube · Math Is Fun: Cylinder · Cone · Sphere. Worked numbers checked by Python. 3:1:2 ratio and volume ×8 scaling: derived, Python-checked.
