Lesson 3 · Angles, triangles and Pythagoras

In one line: angles in a triangle always add to 180°, and in a right triangle the long side squared equals the other two squared added.

1Picture this

A 13 m ladder leans on a wall with its foot 5 m from the wall. How high up the wall does it reach? The wall, ground and ladder make a right-angled triangle, and one rule gives the answer: 12 m.

Ground 5Wall 12 (ladder 13)

Check: 5² + 12² = 25 + 144 = 169 = 13².

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

An angle measures the amount of turn between two arms. A full turn is 360°.
Acute is below 90°. Right is exactly 90°. Obtuse is between 90° and 180°. Straight is exactly 180°. Reflex is above 180°.
Complementary angles add to 90°. Supplementary angles add to 180°. Memory aid: C for corner, S for straight.
The three angles inside any triangle add to 180°.
Extend one side of a triangle outwards: the angle formed outside is an exterior angle. It equals the sum of the two interior angles (the angles inside the triangle, at the other two corners, called vertices) NOT next to it.
Angles in a ratio such as 2 : 3 : 4 (ratio is chapter 05): add the parts (9), divide 180 by that (20 per part), then multiply each part.
In a right triangle (one angle is 90°) the longest side (hypotenuse) is opposite the right angle. The two shorter sides that meet at the right angle are the legs. Rule: a² + b² = c².

3The rules (now that you get it)

Triangle: A + B + C = 180°
Exterior angle = sum of the two far (non-adjacent) interior angles
Complement of x = 90° − x · Supplement of x = 180° − x
Right triangle: a² + b² = c² (c is the hypotenuse)
Rectangle diagonal = √(a² + b²) · Square diagonal = a√2

Definitions and rules as stated on the pages listed in Sources.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Two angles of a triangle are 50° and 60°. Find the third angle and the exterior angle at the third vertex.

Step 1. Third angle = 180 − 50 − 60 = 70°.
Step 2. The exterior angle there = sum of the other two = 50 + 60 = 110°.
Step 3. Check: 180 − 70 = 110.

A ladder 13 m long has its foot 5 m from the wall. Height reached?

Step 1. The ladder is the hypotenuse: c = 13. One leg is 5.
Step 2. Missing leg² = 13² − 5². Here 13 × 13 = 169 and 5 × 5 = 25, so 169 − 25 = 144.
Step 3. √144 = 12 m.

Find the complement and supplement of 35°.

Step 1. Complement = 90 − 35 = 55°.
Step 2. Supplement = 180 − 35 = 145°.

5The trap

Hypotenuse 13, one leg 5. Find the other leg.
Wrong move: add the squares as if finding the hypotenuse: 5² + 13² = 194, √194 is about 13.93. A leg can never be longer than the hypotenuse, so this fails the sense check.
Right: SUBTRACT when a leg is missing. 169 − 25 = 144, leg = 12. Second trap: complement of 40° is 50°, not 140° (that is the supplement).

6Mental-math shortcut: Pythagorean triples

Whole-number right triangles appear again and again. If you spot one, you skip the square roots. Any multiple of a triple is also a triple.

Base tripleDoubled / tripled
3, 4, 56, 8, 10 · 9, 12, 15
5, 12, 1310, 24, 26
8, 15, 1716, 30, 34
7, 24, 2514, 48, 50
20, 21, 29-
Use it. Legs 9 and 12? That is 3 × (3, 4) so hypotenuse = 3 × 5 = 15. No calculation needed.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: Pythagoras and angle helper

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "The angles of a triangle are in the ratio 2 : 3 : 4. Find the largest angle." (180 ÷ 9 = 20 per part, 4 × 20 = 80°.)
  • "A ladder 25 m long reaches a window 24 m high. How far is its foot from the wall?"
  • "Find the length of the diagonal of a rectangle 15 m by 8 m."
  • "An exterior angle of a triangle is 110° and one interior opposite angle is 50°. Find the other."

Geometry questions are not named in the reported 2022 or 2025 breakdowns (only a rectangle question in 2025, reported in memory-based exam analyses). Treat this lesson as lower priority than Lessons 1 and 2.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance is above 20%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What do the angles of a triangle add to? (2) Complementary versus supplementary? (3) Pythagoras rule, and when do I subtract? (4) Name two triples.
Memorise this
1. Triangle angles: 180°.
2. Complement 90°, supplement 180°.
3. Exterior angle = sum of two far interior angles.
4. a² + b² = c²: add for the long side, subtract for a leg.
5. Triples: 3-4-5, 5-12-13, 8-15-17, 7-24-25.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Angles · Complementary and Supplementary · Wikipedia: Triangle · Math Is Fun: Pythagoras. Pythagorean triples table and multiples: derived, Python-checked. 2025 rectangle count: edutap exam analysis (memory-based). Worked numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: parallel-line angle rules, polygon angle sums, circle theorems (not fetched, omitted); how often geometry appears in the paper.

Permutation, Combination, Probability

In one line: first learn to COUNT ways (multiply stages), then chance is just "ways I want ÷ all ways".

Three lessons. Do one lesson, then stop. Lesson 1: arrangements (order matters). Lesson 2: selections (order does not matter). Lesson 3: probability. Permutation, combination and probability are in the third-party Oliveboard topic list (not official). The 2022 and 2025 topic counts reported in memory-based exam analyses (Edutap) do not list a question of this type, so treat this chapter as lower priority than DI, quadratic equations and number series.

Lesson 1 · Counting and arrangements (permutations)

In one line: to count ways, split the job into stages and multiply the choices at each stage.

1Picture this

You are packing for a trip from Lucknow. You own 3 shirts and 4 trousers. Every shirt can go with every trouser. How many different outfits?

Shirt 1: 4Shirt 2: 4Shirt 3: 4

Each shirt has 4 trouser partners. 4 + 4 + 4 = 12, which is 3 × 4.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Break the job into stages. Stage 1: pick a shirt (3 ways). Stage 2: pick a trouser (4 ways).
When each stage can be done in its own number of ways, multiply the numbers. 3 × 4 = 12.
Now arrange 3 people (A, B, C) in 3 chairs. Chair 1: 3 choices. Chair 2: 2 left. Chair 3: 1 left.
So 3 × 2 × 1 = 6 arrangements. That "multiply down to 1" is called a factorial, written 3!.
A permutation is an ordered arrangement. "Gold, silver" is different from "silver, gold".
Choose and arrange only r out of n (n = how many things you pick from, r = how many places you fill): multiply just r terms, starting at n and counting down. This is written nPr, so 10P2 means "arrange 2 out of 10".

3The rules (now that you get it)

Counting principle: multiply the number of ways at each stage.
n! = n × (n−1) × … × 2 × 1, and by convention 0! = 1
nPr = n! ÷ (n−r)! (order matters, no repeats)
Repeats allowed, r places: n × n × … = nr (n multiplied by itself r times, e.g. 104 = 10 × 10 × 10 × 10)

Definitions and formulas: Math Is Fun "Combinations and Permutations" (see Sources).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

10 people run a race. In how many ways can first and second place be filled?

Step 1. Order matters (1st is not 2nd). So this is a permutation: 10P2.
Step 2. First place: 10 choices. Second place: 9 left.
Step 3. 10 × 9 = 90.
Check with the formula: 10! ÷ 8! = 10 × 9 = 90 (the 8 × 7 × … × 1 on top cancels the 8! below).

A 4-digit PIN (the secret number code for a bank card) uses digits 0 to 9. How many PINs if no digit may repeat?

Step 1. Digit 1: 10 choices. Digit 2: 9. Digit 3: 8. Digit 4: 7.
Step 2. 10 × 9 = 90, then 90 × 8 = 720, then 720 × 7 = 5,040 (that is 10P4).
Step 3. If repeats were allowed: 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 10,000.

5The trap

Question: no-repeat 4-digit PIN from digits 0 to 9.
Wrong move: write 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 10,000. That lets a digit be used again.
Right: each used digit leaves the pool, so 10 × 9 × 8 × 7 = 5,040. Always ask: "can the same thing be used twice?"

6Mental-math base: factorials and nPr

Learn the factorial table once. nPr then needs no division: write r terms downward from n.

nn!nn!
015120
116720
2275,040
36840,320
424
Use it. 7P3 = 7 × 6 × 5 = 210 (3 terms because r = 3). 9P2 = 9 × 8 = 72.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: arrangements calculator

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "In how many ways can 5 different books be arranged on a shelf?"
  • "In how many ways can gold, silver and bronze medals be given to 8 runners?"
  • "How many 3-digit codes can be made from digits 0 to 9 if no digit repeats?"

Word clues: "arranged", "in order", "rank", "first, second, third", "code" mean order matters. "No repetition" means the pool shrinks each stage.

How the wrong options are built. The planted answers are usually the repeat-allowed number (nr), the order-ignored number (nCr, a selection count taught in Lesson 2), or a stage you forgot to multiply.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What do I do with stages? (2) What is 5! ? (3) When does order matter? (4) Why is no-repeat 10 × 9 × 8 × 7, not 104?
Memorise this
1. Stages: multiply the choices.
2. n! = n × (n−1) × … × 1, and 0! = 1.
3. nPr (order matters) = n × (n−1) × … (r terms).
4. Repeats allowed: nr.
5. 4! = 24, 5! = 120, 6! = 720, 7! = 5,040.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Combinations and Permutations · Exam facts: PFRDA 2026 notification; topic list: Oliveboard (third-party). All worked numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: arrangements of words with repeated letters (not sourced, so omitted); how many such questions appear in the real paper.

Lesson 2 · Selections (combinations)

In one line: a combination is a selection where order does not matter, so divide the arrangements by r!.

1Picture this

Four friends (A, B, C, D; you are A) want 2 of the group to fetch chai. Choosing "A and B" is the same as choosing "B and A". The same two people go.

ABACADBCBDCD

Only 6 different pairs, not 12.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

A combination is a choice where the order does not matter. nCr means "the number of ways to choose r things out of n".
First count as if order mattered: 4P2 = 4 × 3 = 12.
But each pair got counted 2 times (AB and BA). 2 = 2!.
So divide: 12 ÷ 2 = 6 pairs. In general, nCr = nPr ÷ r!.
Choosing r to take is the same as choosing n−r to leave. So nCr = nC(n−r). Use whichever r is smaller.
Several groups with separate conditions: do each group's selection, then multiply (counting principle).

3The rules (now that you get it)

nCr = n! ÷ [r! × (n−r)!] = nPr ÷ r!
nCr = nC(n−r) (swap r and n−r in the formula and it does not change)
"At least one" = (all selections) − (selections with none)

Combination definition and formula: Math Is Fun "Combinations and Permutations". The "at least one" subtraction is the same idea as the "easier to work out the No case" trick on Math Is Fun "Conditional Probability" (see Sources).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

A committee of 3 is chosen from 5 men and 4 women, exactly 2 men and 1 woman. How many ways?

Step 1. Choose 2 men from 5: 5C2 = (5 × 4) ÷ (2 × 1) = 10.
Step 2. Choose 1 woman from 4: 4C1 = 4 (four people, any one of them).
Step 3. Both stages happen, so multiply: 10 × 4 = 40.

Same 5 men and 4 women, committee of 3 with at least one woman?

Step 1. All committees of 3 from 9 people: 9C3 = (9 × 8 × 7) ÷ (3 × 2 × 1) = 504 ÷ 6 = 84.
Step 2. Committees with NO woman (all men): 5C3 = 5C2 (swap rule) = 10.
Step 3. At least one woman = 84 − 10 = 74.
Check by adding cases. 1 woman: 4C1 × 5C2 = 4 × 10 = 40. 2 women: 4C2 × 5C1 = 6 × 5 = 30. 3 women: 4C3 × 5C0 = 4 × 1 = 4. Total 40 + 30 + 4 = 74.

5The trap

Question: choose a team of 3 from 9 people.
Wrong move: 9 × 8 × 7 = 504. That counts "A, B, C" and "C, B, A" as different teams.
Right: each team is counted 3! = 6 times, so 504 ÷ 6 = 84. If order does not matter, divide by r!.

6Mental-math base: small nCr values

Write r terms down from n on top, r! below. Cancel before you multiply. Use the smaller of r and n−r.

nnC2nC3
51010
61520
72135
82856
93684
1045120
Use it. 10C8 = 10C2 = 45. Handshakes among 8 people, each pair once: 8C2 = 28.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: selections calculator

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A committee of 3 is to be formed from 6 men and 4 women with exactly 2 men. In how many ways?"
  • "From 5 boys and 3 girls, a group of 4 is chosen with at least one girl. How many ways?"
  • "8 people each shake hands with every other person once. How many handshakes?"

Word clues: "committee", "group", "team", "select", "choose" mean order does not matter. "Exactly" means fix the numbers. "At least one" means subtract the none-case.

How the wrong options are built. Expect the nPr value (forgot to divide by r!), the total ignoring the condition, and added instead of multiplied stage counts.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Does order matter in a committee? (2) How do I get nCr from nPr? (3) What is 10C8 the same as? (4) How do I do "at least one"?
Memorise this
1. nCr = nPr ÷ r! = n! ÷ [r!(n−r)!].
2. nCr = nC(n−r): use the smaller r.
3. Separate groups: multiply their nCr values.
4. "At least one" = total − none.
5. nC2: 5→10, 6→15, 7→21, 8→28, 9→36, 10→45.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Combinations and Permutations · Math Is Fun: Conditional Probability. All worked numbers checked by Python. "At least one = total − none" as a counting rule: derived, Python-checked (committee example, both methods give 74).
UNVERIFIED: Pascal's triangle and distribution of identical items (not fetched, so omitted); frequency in the real paper.

Lesson 3 · Probability

In one line: probability = (ways the thing can happen) ÷ (all possible outcomes), always between 0 and 1.

1Picture this

In a game of Ludo you roll one die. Six faces: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6. You need a 6 to bring a token out. One face helps you out of six.

5 faces: no6

Chance of a 6 = 1 out of 6 = 1/6.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

List every possible result of the experiment. This list is the sample space. One die (plural: dice) has faces {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}. "Fair" means every result is equally likely. P(A) is short for "the probability of event A".
An event is one or more of those results. "Even number" = {2, 4, 6}.
Probability = number of ways the event can happen ÷ total number of outcomes. Even: 3 ÷ 6 = 1/2.
It is always from 0 (impossible) to 1 (certain).
Not: P(not A) = 1 − P(A). Often it is easier to count the "No" case and subtract from 1.
And for independent events (one does not change the other, like two coin tosses): multiply. Or for events that cannot happen together (mutually exclusive): add. Drawing "without replacement" (not putting the item back) changes the next chance.
Coin example: P(head) = 1/2. Two tosses both heads: 1/2 × 1/2 = 1/4. Three tosses all heads: 1/8.

3The rules (now that you get it)

P(event) = favourable outcomes ÷ total outcomes
P(not A) = 1 − P(A)
Independent: P(A and B) = P(A) × P(B)
Mutually exclusive: P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B). Otherwise: P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A and B)

Math Is Fun pages on Probability, Independent Events, Mutually Exclusive Events and Conditional Probability (see Sources).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Two fair dice are rolled. Probability that the sum is 7?

Step 1. Sample space: 6 × 6 = 36 equally likely pairs.
Step 2. Sum 7: (1,6) (2,5) (3,4) (4,3) (5,2) (6,1). That is 6 pairs.
Step 3. 6 ÷ 36, divide top and bottom by 6: 1/6.

A bag has 4 red, 3 blue, 2 green balls. Two are drawn without putting back. Probability both are red?

Step 1. Total 9 balls. First red: 4/9.
Step 2. A red is gone: 3 red left among 8 balls. Second red: 3/8.
Step 3. Multiply: 4/9 × 3/8 = 12/72. Divide top and bottom by 12: 1/6.
Check with counting: 4C2 ÷ 9C2 = 6 ÷ 36 = 1/6.

A card is drawn from a pack of 52. Probability it is a heart or a king? (A pack has 4 suits: hearts, diamonds, clubs, spades, 13 cards each. It has 4 kings and 4 queens.)

Step 1. Hearts: 13. Kings: 4. The king of hearts is in both lists.
Step 2. Cards that are heart or king: 13 + 4 − 1 = 16.
Step 3. 16 ÷ 52, divide top and bottom by 4: 4/13.

5The trap

Question: two dice, probability the sum is 7.
Wrong move: "sums go from 2 to 12, that is 11 results, so 1/11". But the 11 sums are not equally likely.
Right: list the 36 equally likely pairs. Sum 7 appears 6 times: 6/36 = 1/6. Count outcomes that are equally likely, not labels.

6Mental-math base: two-dice sums

Out of 36 pairs. The count rises 1 to 6, then falls back. Memorise the shape.

SumWaysSumWays
2185
3294
43103
54112
65121
76
Use it. Sum 10 or more = 3 + 2 + 1 = 6 ways, so 6/36 = 1/6. King or queen: 4 + 4 = 8 cards (no card is both), so 8/52 = 2/13. Deck facts you need: 52 cards, 4 kings, 13 hearts.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: two-dice sum

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A fair die is thrown. What is the probability of getting a number greater than 4?"
  • "Two dice are thrown together. Find the probability that the sum is 8."
  • "A bag contains 4 red, 3 blue and 2 green balls. Two balls are drawn without replacement. Probability both are red?"
  • "A coin is tossed three times. Probability of at least one head?"

Word clues: "without replacement" means the second chance changes. "At least one" means 1 − P(none). "Or" means add (subtract the overlap). "And", "together", "both" mean multiply or count together.

How the wrong options are built. Expect the with-replacement answer, the answer to the complement (the "none" case), and the double-counted overlap.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Guess only if your chance of being right is above 20%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What is the probability formula? (2) What is the range of probability? (3) How do I do "at least one"? (4) With or without replacement: which changes the second chance?
Memorise this
1. P = favourable ÷ total, between 0 and 1.
2. P(not A) = 1 − P(A).
3. Independent AND: multiply. Exclusive OR: add. Overlap OR: subtract the overlap.
4. Two dice: 36 outcomes; sum 7 has 6 ways.
5. Cards: 52 total, 4 kings, 13 hearts.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Probability · Independent Events · Mutually Exclusive Events · Conditional Probability. All worked numbers checked by Python (two-dice counts by full enumeration).

Data Interpretation I: Tables, Bar, Line

Three lessons, three full 5-question practice sets (Sets A, B, C). One lesson at a time, then stop.

Lesson 1: Reading tables

In one line: a table gives you exact numbers; your job is to pick the two you need, decide which one is the OLD (base), and do one small calculation.

1Picture this

You open the passbook page of a pension subscriber. Each row is one year, each column one fact: contribution, interest, balance. To answer "how much did the balance grow?" you do not read every number. You find the two cells you need and ignore the rest.

YearContribution (₹)Balance (₹)
Year 112,00012,000
Year 212,00025,000

Ask first: which row, which column, which one is the old value? Everything else is noise.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Read the title and the unit first (₹, thousands, per cent). A wrong unit ruins a right sum. (A series is one row or one coloured set of values, like "Office A" or "Product X".)
Read the question and underline the two things asked: which row(s), which column(s).
Circle only those cells. Ignore the rest of the table.
Decide the type: a share (part ÷ total), a change (new − old ÷ old), or a ratio (A : B).
For "than" and "from … to …", the base is the old / "than" value. Divide by it.
Estimate first (round to friendly numbers), then pick the option that matches. Exact long division is rarely needed.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Share % = Part ÷ Total × 100
Change % = (New − Old) ÷ Old × 100
Ratio A : B = A ÷ their HCF : B ÷ their HCF

HCF = the biggest number that divides both exactly (chapter 10). Example: HCF of 90 and 42 is 6, because 90 = 6 × 15 and 42 = 6 × 7.

Percentage change formula: Math Is Fun, "Percentage Change" (see Sources). Share is the same percentage idea with the total as base. Ratios: divide both numbers by the same value (Math Is Fun, "Ratios"); dividing by the HCF gives lowest terms (derived, Python-checked).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Office P received 250 forms in 2023 and 300 in 2024. It rejected 30 of the 2024 forms. Find the growth and the rejected share.

Practice data, invented for this guide. Not from any real exam.

Step 1. Growth: old = 250, new = 300. Change = 50.
Step 2. Divide by OLD: 50 ÷ 250 = 1/5 = 20%.
Step 3. Rejected share uses the 2024 total as base: 30 ÷ 300 = 1/10 = 10%.
Answer: 20% growth, 10% rejected. Two questions, two different bases.

5The trap

Question: "Office E: 350 forms in 2023, 420 in 2024. What is the % growth?" Change = 70.
Wrong move: divide by the NEW number. 70 ÷ 420 = 16.67%. This is exactly the option the examiner plants.
Right: divide by the OLD number. 70 ÷ 350 = 20%.
A second trap: the office with the biggest rise in numbers is not always the office with the biggest % growth. Compare like with like.

6Mental-math: friendly fractions, then compare

Turn every percentage into a fraction you know. Chunk one: 1/2, 1/4, 1/5, 1/8, 1/10.

Fraction%Fraction%
1/812.51/616.67
1/714.291/911.11
1/5201/425
Use it. 70 out of 350 is 1/5, so 20%. 72 out of 576 is 1/8, so 12.5%. Look for a small whole-number relationship before you divide.

Speed drill

Say it out loud first. Aim: under 5 seconds.

7Lab: one table, three answers

Type two cells of a table to see growth, share and ratio, with steps.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "What is the percentage increase in forms received by Office C from 2023 to 2024?"
  • "Rejected forms of Office A are what percent of its forms received in 2024?"
  • "What is the ratio of rejected forms of Office D to Office E?"
  • "Which office showed the highest percentage growth?"

Reported in memory-based exam analyses: DI was 8 questions (two sets, 5 + 3) in the 2025 Phase I and 5 questions in 2022. Table-type sets are among the formats typically listed by coaching sites (third-party list, not official).

Wrong options are built from the wrong base, the raw change read as a percent, the inverted ratio, and the wrong year. Name the base before you calculate.

9Quiz · full DI Set A (5 questions)

Practice data, invented for this guide. Not from any real exam.

OfficeReceived 2023Received 2024Rejected 2024
A48057672
B65059860
C20026039
D72079290
E35042042

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Break-even guess odds: 20% (the chance of being right at which blind guessing neither gains nor loses marks). Suggested plan, not official: about 6 minutes for a 5-question set.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What do I read first? (2) Which number is the base for growth? (3) Which is the base for a share? (4) Does the biggest rise in numbers mean the biggest % growth?
Memorise this
1. Title and unit first.
2. Circle only the two cells you need.
3. Growth = change ÷ OLD.
4. Share = part ÷ total.
5. Compare % growth with %, not with raw rises.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Percentage Change · Math Is Fun: Ratios. Break-even guess odds 20% (1.25p = 0.3125(1 − p)): derived, Python-checked. Table-type DI listed as a format by coaching sites (third-party). DI counts: edutap exam analysis (memory-based).

Lesson 2: Bar charts

In one line: a bar chart is a table drawn with bar heights, so read the axis and unit, write the bar values into a mini-table, then calculate as before.

1Picture this

A chai stall owner draws how many cups he sold each day this week. Taller bar, more cups. He does not need numbers to see which day was best, but the exam asks "how many more", so you must read the value off the scale.

Mon 40
Tue 60
Wed 80

Wed − Mon = 40 more cups. Wed is double Mon.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

A bar graph shows data with bars of different heights, so you can compare at a glance (Math Is Fun).
Read the title, then both axes (the axis is the numbered line along the side or bottom): what is measured and in which unit (units, thousands, ₹ crore; 1 crore = 1,00,00,000).
Check the legend (the small key that says which colour is which series).
Write every bar value you need into a tiny table on your rough sheet. Do this once.
Now it is a table question. Pick the formula: share, change or ratio.
Gap questions (bar A minus bar B in the same year) compare the heights directly. Do not use % unless asked.
"Absolute difference" just means the gap with the sign ignored: always big minus small.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Read the value → write a mini-table → then calculate
Average (mean) = Sum of values ÷ How many values
A is what % more than B = (A − B) ÷ B × 100

Bar graph: Math Is Fun "Bar Graphs"; Mean: Math Is Fun "How to Calculate the Mean". Good bar graphs begin the axis at zero (Math Is Fun), so check the axis before trusting bar lengths.

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Using the chart below (Set B data): X sold 55 thousand in 2021 and 65 thousand in 2023. Find the % growth of X from 2021 to 2023.

Practice data, invented for this guide. Not from any real exam.

■ Product X   ■ Product Y   (units sold, in thousands)

Step 1. Read the bars: X 2021 = 55, X 2023 = 65. Unit: thousands.
Step 2. Change = 65 − 55 = 10.
Step 3. Divide by OLD (2021): 10 ÷ 55 = 2/11 (divide top and bottom by 5).
Step 4. 2 ÷ 11 = 0.1818, so 18.18%. Answer: about 18.2%. Estimate check: 55 × 1.2 = 66, just above 65, so a bit under 20%.

5The trap

The chart says "in thousands". Bar reads 60. A careless answer writes "60" for "how many units".
Right: 60 thousand = 60,000. If options are 60 and 60,000, the unit decides.
Second trap: "gap" is a subtraction, "how much % more" is a division by the base.
Year 2022: Y = 60, X = 50. Gap = 10. Y is 10 ÷ 50 = 20% more than X (base X, the "than" value).
But X is only 10 ÷ 60 = 16.67% less than Y (base Y). Different base, different answer.

6Mental-math: average by "guess and nudge"

Average of 40, 55, 50, 65, 80. Guess 60. Write each distance from 60: −20, −5, −10, +5, +20. Add them: −10. Divide by 5 values: −2. Average = 60 − 2 = 58. Check: sum 290 ÷ 5 = 58.

The median is the middle value when sorted (here 55). It is not the mean. Examiners use it as a wrong option.

Why it works. Each value = guess + distance, so the average = guess + average distance. Small numbers, no big addition.

Speed drill

Say it out loud first.

7Lab: average by guess and nudge

Enter five bar values and a guess. See the distances.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "What is the average sales of Product X over the five years?"
  • "In which year was the difference between the sales of X and Y the highest?"
  • "Sales of Y in 2022 are what percent more than sales of X in 2022?"
  • "What is the ratio of Y in 2021 to X in 2023?"

Reported in memory-based exam analyses: DI was the biggest block in 2025 (8 questions, 5 + 3) and joint-biggest in 2022 (5 questions, tied with Number Series). Bar graph is listed among DI types in third-party topic lists (not official).

Wrong options are built from the highest bar instead of the biggest gap, the median instead of the mean, and the unit slip.

9Quiz · full DI Set B (5 questions)

Practice data, invented for this guide. Not from any real exam.

■ Product X   ■ Product Y   (units sold, in thousands)

Values are printed on the bars. In the exam you must read them off the axis, so practise estimating to the nearest gridline.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What do I check before reading any bar? (2) What is the mean formula? (3) "A is what % more than B": which is the base? (4) Why does the unit matter?
Memorise this
1. Title, axes, unit, legend.
2. Copy needed bars into a mini-table.
3. Mean = sum ÷ count (guess and nudge).
4. "More than B" means divide by B.
5. Gap is subtraction. % is division.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Bar Graphs · Math Is Fun: Mean · Math Is Fun: Percentage Change Guess-and-nudge average (mean = guess + mean of distances): derived, Python-checked.

Lesson 3: Line charts and percent change

In one line: a line chart shows change over time; the slope between two points is the story, and every "growth" question is old-to-new percent change.

1Picture this

You track your daily steps for a week. Joining the dots shows a line going up, down, up. A line graph shows information connected in some way, usually as it changes over time (Math Is Fun). The exam asks: between which two days did it rise most, in per cent?

Day 1: 80
Day 2: 100
Day 3: 90

Up 20, then down 10. Different bases, so different percents.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Read the title, unit and legend. Note if the axis starts at zero (the axis is the numbered scale; the legend tells which line is which series). If it starts higher, small changes look bigger than they are (Math Is Fun gives this warning for bar graphs; it applies to any axis).
Read each point you need and write it in a mini-table, one row per series.
Percent change between two years: (new − old) ÷ old × 100.
The steepest looking part of a line (its slope, how sharply it climbs) is the biggest raw rise, not always the biggest percent rise.
To find the biggest percent rise, test each interval with friendly fractions (1/5 = 20%, 1/4 = 25%).
A change in a percentage is measured in percentage points (the plain difference), not in percent.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Change % = (New − Old) ÷ Old × 100
Percentage points = (second percentage) − (first percentage)
Example: 40% to 44% is +4 percentage points, and a 10% rise in that quantity

Percentage point definition and the 40% to 44% example: Wikipedia "Percentage point". Line graph: Math Is Fun "Line Graphs".

4Worked examples, one tap per step

A line goes 80 → 100 → 90 (three years, invented). Find the % change in each step and overall.

Practice data, invented for this guide. Not from any real exam.

Step 1. 80 → 100: change 20, ÷ 80 = 1/4 = 25% rise.
Step 2. 100 → 90: change −10, ÷ 100 = 10% fall.
Step 3. Overall 80 → 90: change 10, ÷ 80 = 1/8 = 12.5% rise.
Check: 1.25 × 0.90 = 1.125. The two steps do not add (25 − 10 = 15 is wrong).

5The trap

Growth rate = the % growth in a year. It was 10% one year and 25% the next. "By how much did the growth rate rise?"
Wrong move: 25 ÷ 10 = 2.5, so "150%" or "2.5". That is the relative change of the rates (one rate divided by the other), which is not what was asked.
Right: when the question is about percentages themselves, subtract: 25 − 10 = 15 percentage points.

6Mental-math: the growth ladder

Memorise these pairs. They turn most intervals into one glance.

Old → NewChange %
×1.1 (e.g. 200 → 220)+10%
×1.25 (e.g. 80 → 100)+25%
×0.8 (e.g. 220 → 176)−20%
×0.9 (e.g. 100 → 90)−10%
Use it. 220 → 176: 176 ÷ 220 = 0.8, so a 20% fall. And to undo +25% you need −20% (1/4 up, 1/5 down). To undo +10% you need about −9.09%, because the new value is 11 parts and you remove 1 of them: 1 ÷ 11 = 9.09%.

Speed drill

Say it out loud first.

7Lab: percent change vs percentage points

Enter two rates (in %).

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "What is the percentage decrease in Line P from 2020 to 2021?"
  • "In which period did Line P show the highest percentage rise?"
  • "What is the average of Line Q for the last three years?"
  • "By how many percentage points did the growth rate increase?"
  • "What is the ratio of P in 2024 to Q in 2020?"

Reported in memory-based exam analyses: DI was the biggest block in 2025 (8 questions) and joint-biggest in 2022 (5 questions, tied with Number Series). Line graph is listed among DI types in third-party topic lists (not official). Pie, caselet and radar are in the next chapter.

Wrong options are built from the biggest absolute rise, dividing by the new value, the lost sign, and relative change used instead of points.

9Quiz · full DI Set C (5 questions)

Practice data, invented for this guide. Not from any real exam.

■ Line P   ■ Line Q   (enrolments, in thousands; axis starts at 100, not 0)

Year201920202021202220232024
P200220176240264330
Q150180225210270297

The table repeats the line values so you can check the picture.

Suggested plan, not official: about 6 minutes for a 5-question set.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Which value is the base for % change? (2) Is the steepest line the biggest % rise? (3) What do you do to get percentage points? (4) What does a line chart show best?
Memorise this
1. Line = change over time.
2. % change = change ÷ OLD.
3. Biggest rise in numbers is not biggest %.
4. Rates compared: subtract, answer in points.
5. +25% is undone by −20%; +10% is undone by about −9.09%.

Sources: Bar Graphs · Line Graphs · Percentage Change · Mean · Ratios (Math Is Fun) · Wikipedia: Percentage point · PFRDA 2026 notification (marks) · DI counts: edutap exam analysis, reported in memory-based exam analyses. All practice data invented; every answer checked by Python.

Data Interpretation II: pie, caselet, radar, mixed

In one line: turn every chart or paragraph into a small table, then use percent and ratio.

Lesson 1 · Pie charts: percent, degrees, and two-chart sets

In one line: a pie is a 360° circle, so 1% = 3.6°, and every slice answer is "slice × total".

1Picture this

A chai stall owner in Pune cuts a round cake into slices for 100 customers. A slice for 25 customers is a quarter of the cake, so the slice angle is a quarter turn: 90° (a full turn is 360°, and 360 ÷ 4 = 90).

Bigger share, bigger angle. Total of all shares = whole cake = 360° = 100%.

2The idea in small steps

A pie chart shows how one total is split into parts.
The whole circle is 360° and also 100% of the total.
So 1% of the total sits in 360 ÷ 100 = 3.6° of the circle.
Percent to degrees: multiply by 3.6. Degrees to percent: divide by 3.6.
To get a real count: percent of the total, or angle ÷ 360 × total. (The slice angle at the centre is also called the central angle.)
A table may give "women %" for each slice. The rest are men: men % = 100% − women %.
Two-chart sets: the pie gives the share, a table gives a second percent. Do share first, then percent of that share.

3The rule

Slice angle = (part ÷ total) × 360° = percent × 3.6°
Count = (percent ÷ 100) × total = (angle ÷ 360) × total

Sector angle for fraction Q = 360Q degrees: Wikipedia: Pie chart (sector angle = 360 × fraction).

4Worked example, one tap per step

Practice data, invented. 12,000 new pension-scheme enrolments in a month, split by state (pie below). Women are 40% of state A's enrolments. How many women in A?

StateShare of 12,000Women in that state
A30%40%
B20%55%
C15%60%
D25%20%
E10%50%
Step 1. A's share = 30% of 12,000.
Step 2. 30% = 3/10, so 12,000 ÷ 10 × 3 = 3,600 enrolments.
Step 3. Women = 40% of 3,600 = 3,600 ÷ 10 × 4 = 1,440.
Step 4. Answer: 1,440 women. Angle check: A slice = 30 × 3.6 = 108°.

5The trap

A question asks for "women as % of all 12,000" and the table shows 40, 55, 60, 20, 50.
Wrong move: average them: (40+55+60+20+50) ÷ 5 = 45%.
Right move: states have different sizes, so find each state's women first. A 1,440 (from the example). B: 20% of 12,000 = 2,400, 55% of 2,400 = 1,320. C: 15% = 1,800, 60% of 1,800 = 1,080. D: 25% = 3,000, 20% of 3,000 = 600. E: 10% = 1,200, 50% of 1,200 = 600.
Add them: 1,440+1,320+1,080+600+600 = 5,040. 5,040 ÷ 12,000 = 42%. (Ratio basics: chapter 19, lesson 1.)

6Mental-math shortcut: percent ↔ degrees

PercentAngle
1%3.6°
5%18°
10%36°
12.5%45°
20%72°
25%90°
50%180°
Use it. 15% = 10% + 5% = 36 + 18 = 54°. Build any slice from 10% and 5% blocks.

Speed drill

Say it out loud first.

7Lab: pie angle calculator

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "The pie shows share by state. Find the central angle for state X."
  • "If the total is N, how many are from Y?"
  • "A table gives the % of women in each state. How many women in Z?"
  • "Find the ratio of men in A to men in E."

DI was reported as the biggest block in 2025 (8 questions: 5+3) in memory-based exam analyses.

Wrong options are built from: the plain average of percents, the angle of a neighbouring slice, men instead of women, and a wrong base.

9Quiz · Set A (5 questions, practice data, invented)

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Set data is practice data, invented. Use your own paper to work; check each step.

12,000 enrolments, pie of state shares above, women % by state in the table above.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Degrees in a circle? (2) Degrees per 1%? (3) Why is the average of five percents risky? (4) Angle for 25%?
Memorise this
1. Circle = 360° = 100%.
2. 1% = 3.6°.
3. Count = percent × total.
4. Two-chart: share first, then percent of it.
5. Never average percents of unequal groups.

Sources: Wikipedia: Pie chart · Data: invented by us. All numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: that pie-with-table sets appear in this exact form (not claimed).

Lesson 2 · Caselets: turn words into a table

In one line: a caselet is a data table written as a paragraph, so draw the table first and the questions become easy.

1Picture this

Your neighbour describes the sabzi bill in words: "I bought tomatoes and onions, onions cost twice the tomatoes..." You cannot add that in your head. You jot it down in rows. A caselet needs the same jotting.

2The idea in small steps

Read the whole paragraph once and underline every number.
Draw a table: one row per item (machine, branch, person), one column per fact.
Fill the facts. Turn "the rest" into 100% minus the others.
Turn each percent into a real count, using the right total.
Add a "check" cell: parts must add back to the total.
Only then read the questions.

3The rule

Defective = (share × total) × defect rate. Non-defective = made − defective.
"Rest" = whole − sum of the named parts.
One machine's share of all defects = its defective ÷ total defective.

Defective = faulty, not usable. Non-defective = good.

Overall rate = total defective ÷ total made (never the average of the rates).

These follow from the definition of a percentage (Wikipedia: Percentage); derived, Python-checked.

4Worked example, one tap per step

Practice data, invented. A printing unit prints 5,000 ID cards in a day on three machines. M1 prints 40% of the day's cards, M2 prints 35% and M3 the rest. Of the cards from M1, 2% are defective; from M2, 4%; from M3, 6%. How many defective cards in all?

Step 1. M3 share = 100 − 40 − 35 = 25%.
Step 2. Cards: M1 = 40% of 5,000 = 2,000. M2 = 35% = 1,750. M3 = 25% = 1,250. Check: 2,000+1,750+1,250 = 5,000.
Step 3. Defective: M1 2% of 2,000 = 40. M2 4% of 1,750 = 70. M3 6% of 1,250 = 75.
Step 4. Total = 40+70+75 = 185.

MachineMadeDefect rateDefectiveNon-defective
M12,0002%401960
M21,7504%701680
M31,2506%751175
Total5,0001854815

5The trap

Question: overall defective %. Rates are 2%, 4%, 6%.
Wrong move: average them to get 4%.
Right move: M1 made more than M3, so each rate must count for as many cards as it covers (this is "weighting"). Total defective ÷ total made: 185 ÷ 5,000 = 3.7%.

6Mental-math shortcut: percent of a round number

Split the percent into 1% and 10% blocks of the number. 1% of 1,750 is 17.5, so 4% = 70. 1% of 1,250 is 12.5, so 6% = 75.

Speed drill

7Lab: defective calculator

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "A factory has three units... find the total defective."
  • "What percent of all items are non-defective?"
  • "Find the ratio of defective items of unit X to unit Z."

For 2025, one complex DI set on defective and non-defective manufactured products was reported in memory-based exam analyses (edutap). Its exact data and format are not known.

Wrong options are built from: the average of rates, production ratio instead of defect ratio, and defective vs non-defective swapped.

9Quiz · Set B (5 questions, practice data, invented)

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Set data is practice data, invented. Use your own paper to work; check each step.

A printing unit prints 5,000 ID cards in a day on three machines. M1 prints 40% of the day's cards, M2 prints 35% and M3 the rest. Of the cards from M1, 2% are defective; from M2, 4%; from M3, 6%.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What is the first thing to draw? (2) How do I find "the rest"? (3) Why not average the rates?
Memorise this
1. Draw the table first.
2. Rest = 100% − named parts.
3. Defective = share × total × rate.
4. Overall rate = defective ÷ total.
5. Check: parts add to whole.

Sources: Wikipedia: Percentage · Data invented by us. All numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: the exact data and format of the 2025 defective/non-defective set (only its topic is reported, single source: edutap exam analysis, memory-based).

Lesson 3 · Radar charts and mixed sets

In one line: a radar chart is a line graph bent into a circle, so read each spoke like a bar and compare the two shapes.

1Picture this

A cricket coach scores two batters on six skills and joins the dots on a spider web. A bigger shape means better overall, but each spoke is still just one number.

2The idea in small steps

A radar chart has spokes from one centre, each spoke is one category.
Spokes are equally spaced. Here 6 spokes are 60° apart.
Distance from the centre is the value. Rings show the scale.
Each series (one branch, one colour) is one outline joined across spokes.
Read the value of a point from the ring it touches.
Then it is a normal table: write both series in a row and use percent and average rules.

3The rule

Radar chart: three or more measured quantities (here, months), each on its own spoke, all spokes starting from the same centre.
Percent difference "A over B" = (A − B) ÷ B × 100.
Percent fall = (Old − New) ÷ Old × 100.
Average = sum ÷ how many values (chapter 19, lesson 2).

Radar definition: Wikipedia: Radar chart. Percent change is measured from the original (base) value: Wikipedia: Percentage.

4Worked example, one tap per step

Practice data, invented. Monthly enrolments (hundreds) at two branches, January to June.

■ Branch P   ■ Branch Q   (rings = hundreds of enrolments; each ring is 10)

BranchJanFebMarAprMayJun
P405535605045
Q304550406555

In which month is the gap between P and Q the largest?

Step 1. Read both rows off the table.
Step 2. Gaps: Jan 10, Feb 10, Mar 15, Apr 20, May 15, Jun 10.
Step 3. Largest is April (20), P above Q.

5The trap

Radar shapes look big or small, so you may judge by area.
Wrong move: "Q's shape looks a little bigger, so Q is higher in every month." In fact P and Q both total 285 (P: 40+55+35+60+50+45; Q: 30+45+50+40+65+55), and each is higher in only 3 of 6 months.
Right move: compare spoke by spoke, month by month.

6Mental-math shortcut: gap over base

Write the gap, then the base (the number after "than"), then reduce. 20 over 40 = 1/2 = 50%. 15 over 60 = 1/4 = 25%.

Speed drill

7Reading checklist

  1. Name each series and its colour.
  2. Note the scale unit (hundreds here).
  3. Copy values into a mini table.
  4. Answer from the table, not the picture.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "In how many months is A more than B?"
  • "By what percent is A above B in month X?"
  • "Find the average over six months."
  • "Find the percent fall from month X to month Y."

Radar is on the third-party topic list (Oliveboard), not official.

Wrong options are built from: dividing by the wrong base, giving the raw gap as the percent, and ratios flipped.

9Quiz · Set C (5 questions, practice data, invented)

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Set data is practice data, invented. Use your own paper to work; check each step.

Use the radar chart and table above (hundreds of enrolments).

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What does each spoke show? (2) Which base for "A above B"? (3) Do I answer from the picture or table?
Memorise this
1. Each spoke = one category.
2. Distance from centre = value.
3. Copy points to a table.
4. % = gap ÷ base × 100.
5. Compare spoke by spoke, not by area.

Sources: Wikipedia: Radar chart · Wikipedia: Percentage · Third-party topic list: Oliveboard (not official) · Data invented. All numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: how often radar charts appear in the real paper.

Sources: Wikipedia: Pie chart · Wikipedia: Radar chart · 2025 DI count (8 = 5+3) and the defective/non-defective DI topic: edutap exam analysis, reported in memory-based exam analyses. All data practice data, invented.
UNVERIFIED: exact data/format of the 2025 defective/non-defective set; radar frequency in the paper.

Data Sufficiency

In one line: you do not solve the question, you only decide whether the statements give enough to solve it.

LOW priority. Why this chapter, honestly. Data Sufficiency appears only on a third-party topic list (Oliveboard, not official). The official PFRDA notification lists no Quant topics. In the 2025 and 2022 question counts reported in memory-based exam analyses, Data Sufficiency had zero questions in both years. We have no evidence it is tested. Do this chapter last, only after the high-count topics (DI, quadratic equations, number series, arithmetic), as a short insurance skill.

Lesson 1 · The method: one statement at a time

In one line: test statement I alone, then II alone, and only if both fail, test them joined.

1Picture this

Your friend texts: "How much did the auto cost?" Message 1: "It was double the bus fare." Message 2: "The bus fare was ₹15." Alone, neither tells you the cost. Together, 2 × 15 = ₹30. Data Sufficiency is exactly this: do you have enough to answer, from message 1, from message 2, or only from both?

Notice you never needed to be asked for the answer at the end. You only needed to know whether it could be found.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

You get a question and two statements, I and II.
Question type A (value): "What is x?" (x is just a letter standing for an unknown number.) Enough means exactly one value comes out.
Question type B (yes/no): "Is N even?" Enough means the answer is always yes or always no. A definite NO is also enough. "Sometimes" is not enough.
Read the question, then cover II. Does I alone give enough? Write yes or no.
Now cover I. Does II alone give enough? Write yes or no. Do not borrow anything from the other statement.
Only if both were "no", join them. Now is it enough?
Your two yes/no notes and the joined check decide the answer. That final answer is called the verdict.

3The rules (now that you get it)

I yes, II no: answer is "I alone". I no, II yes: "II alone".
I yes AND II yes: "Either alone is enough".
I no, II no, joined yes: "Both together". Joined no: "Even together, not enough".

Our summary (derived from the method above, not from any PFRDA paper): with two statements there are exactly these five possible verdicts. Real papers word and order the options in their own way, and some bank-exam Data Sufficiency sets use three statements (I, II, III) instead of two (seen on an Oliveboard practice page, see Sources). Read the directions carefully on the day. The quizzes here show four of the five at a time to fit four boxes.

I alone?II alone?Joined?Verdict
YesNo-I alone
NoYes-II alone
YesYes-Either alone
NoNoYesBoth together
NoNoNoNot enough

Definition of the task (decide whether enough information is given): Wikipedia "Graduate Management Admission Test", data sufficiency section (see Sources).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Value question. What is the price of one pen? I. 5 pens cost ₹60. II. A pen costs ₹2 less than a pencil.

Step 1. Cover II. From I: 60 ÷ 5 = 12. One value. I alone is enough.
Step 2. Cover I. From II: pen = pencil − 2, pencil price unknown. Many values. II alone fails.
Step 3. I yes, II no. Verdict: I alone. (We stop. Joining is not needed.)

Yes/no question. Is N divisible by 3? I. N is divisible by 6. II. N is even.

Words: "divisible by 3" means 3 goes in exactly with nothing left over. A multiple of 6 is 6 × a whole number (6, 12, 18...). Even means divisible by 2. Even + 1 is odd, odd + 1 is even.

Step 1. I: a multiple of 6 is 6 × something, which contains 3. So always YES. Enough.
Step 2. II: 4 is even (not divisible by 3), 6 is even (divisible by 3). Sometimes yes, sometimes no. Not enough.
Step 3. Verdict: I alone. If I had said "N is not divisible by 3" the answer would be a definite NO, still enough.

5The trap

How old is Ravi? I. Ravi is 5 years older than Meena. II. Meena is 20.
Wrong move: while checking I, you remember "Meena is 20" from II, so you say "I alone: 20 + 5 = 25". That used II's facts inside I's test.
I by itself: Ravi = Meena + 5, Meena unknown. Not enough. II by itself: Meena only, Ravi never mentioned. Not enough.
Joined: 20 + 5 = 25. Correct verdict: Both together. Wrong verdict from borrowing: "I alone".

6Mental-math shortcut: the "5 verdicts" recall

Do not compute, label. Learn the shortcut question for each statement: "Does this give ONE value (or one definite yes/no)?" Say yes or no in under 10 seconds, then move on.

Earlier-chapter facts the quizzes use. Speed = distance ÷ time (chapter 16). Profit needs selling price SP and cost price CP (chapter 07). Average = sum ÷ how many (chapter 06).
Fast flags for "No". A statement fails alone if: (a) it has fewer facts than unknowns, (b) it is a square (like x² = 16, taught in Lesson 2) or a "less than / more than" statement leaving several values, (c) it talks about a different thing from what is asked.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Picture: the decision ladder

StepDo thisWrite
1Read the question. Value or yes/no?V or YN
2Cover II. Test I alone.Y / N
3Cover I. Test II alone.Y / N
4If both N, join them and test.Y / N
5Match the table in card 3.Verdict

Hold the page at arm's length: you should be able to recite five steps. That is the whole chapter.

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "Question: What is the average of five numbers? I. Their sum is 100. II. The smallest is 12." (I alone.)
  • "Is x positive? I. x² > 0. II. x³ > 0."
  • "What is the CP (cost price) of the article? I. Profit is 20%. II. SP (selling price) is ₹600." (Together; see chapter 07.)
  • Typical format: after the statements come the verdict options. Data Sufficiency is on a third-party topic list (Oliveboard) only; it had no questions in the 2025 or 2022 counts reported in memory-based exam analyses. LOW priority.
How the wrong options are built. The tempting wrong choice is "both together" when one statement was enough, or "I alone" because you borrowed a fact from II.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official 1/4 negative rule). Guessing breaks even at a 20% chance, so guess only when your chance is above that. Each question here shows four of the five verdicts.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) What is "enough" for a value question? (2) Is a definite NO enough for a yes/no question? (3) May I use II while testing I? (4) What verdict when each alone works?
Memorise this
1. Test I alone, then II alone, then join.
2. Enough = one value, or always yes, or always no.
3. Never borrow between statements before joining.
4. Both work alone = "either".
5. Do not solve fully: stop once you know.

Sources: Wikipedia: GMAT (definition of data sufficiency only); Oliveboard: Data Sufficiency questions (three-statement format exists). The five-verdict table and the 20% break-even are derived, Python-checked. Exam facts: PFRDA 2026 notification. Third-party topic list: Oliveboard (not official). Past-paper counts: reported in memory-based exam analyses (Edutap). All worked numbers checked by Python.
UNVERIFIED: exact PFRDA option wording and whether the paper uses 4 or 5 options; whether Data Sufficiency appears in Phase I at all (zero questions in the 2025 and 2022 memory-based counts).

Lesson 2 · Algebra statements: equations, squares, yes/no

In one line: count the DIFFERENT clues against the unknowns, and watch for squares that give two answers.

1Picture this

Two friends split a chai bill at a stall. Clue 1: "Together we paid ₹10." Clue 2: "I paid ₹2 more than you." One clue says only the total. The other says only the gap. Both give you each person's share: ₹6 and ₹4.

₹6₹4

Total 10, difference 2. Two different facts, two unknowns: enough.

2The idea, one tiny step at a time

Each statement is a clue about unknowns such as x and y.
Two unknowns usually need two different clues to pin both down.
"Different" matters. "x + y = 3" and "2x + 2y = 6" say the same thing. The second gives no new information.
Two clues that contradict each other (x + y = 3 and x + y = 9) have no solution at all.
One clue, one unknown: undo the steps backwards. 3x + 2 = 14 gives 3x = 12 (take away 2), then x = 4 (divide by 3).
Squares are sneaky: x² (x times x) = 16 gives two values, 4 and −4, unless another clue removes one. A cube x³ keeps the sign: x³ > 0 means x is positive, but x² > 0 only says x is not 0 (x could be −3).
If the question asks for x + y, you may not need x and y separately. Check what is actually asked. Example: 2x + 2y = 14, divide by 2, x + y = 7.
A prime number has only 1 and itself as factors (2, 3, 5, 7, 11). A number like 9 = 3 × 3 is not prime. For rectangles: area = length × breadth, perimeter = 2 × (length + breadth) (chapter 17).

3The rules (now that you get it)

Unknowns to find = number of different clues needed (usually).
Clues that are multiples of each other count as ONE clue.
Square statement: expect two values (plus and minus).

Dependent equations give no new information; inconsistent ones have no solution: Math Is Fun "Systems of Linear Equations" (see Sources).

4Worked examples, one tap per step

Find x. I. x + y = 10. II. x − y = 2.

Step 1. I alone: x + y = 10 has many pairs (1, 9), (5, 5), (6, 4). Not enough.
Step 2. II alone: x − y = 2 has many pairs (3, 1), (6, 4). Not enough.
Step 3. Join: add the two clues. (x + y) + (x − y) = 10 + 2, the y terms cancel, so 2x = 12 and x = 6. Then y = 10 − 6 = 4.
Step 4. Verdict: Both together.

Is x > 5? I. x² = 36. II. x > 0.

Step 1. I: x = 6 (yes, bigger than 5) or x = −6 (no). Mixed. Not enough.
Step 2. II: x = 1 gives no, x = 10 gives yes. Mixed. Not enough.
Step 3. Join: x = ±6 and positive leaves x = 6. 6 > 5, always yes.
Step 4. Verdict: Both together.

5The trap

Find x. I. x + y = 3. II. 2x + 2y = 6.
Wrong move: "two statements, two unknowns, so together they must be enough". That counts statements, not different information.
Divide II by 2 and you get x + y = 3, exactly I. So the pair is one clue wearing two coats.
x = 0, y = 3 and x = 1, y = 2 both fit. Correct verdict: Even together, not enough.

6Mental-math shortcut: add or subtract the two clues

When both clues are like "x + y = a" and "x − y = b", add them: 2x = a + b. Subtract them: 2y = a − b. This is the quickest mental route.

Use it. x + y = 10 and x − y = 4: add, 2x = 14, x = 7. Then y = 3. Small numbers only: you are checking, not solving to the end.

Speed drill

Say the answer out loud before you tap Show.

7Lab: do two equations pin down x and y?

Type the numbers of two equations: a·x + b·y = c. Watch the verdict.

Try 1, 1, 3 and 2, 2, 6 (same clue). Try 1, 1, 3 and 1, 1, 9 (contradiction).

8How the examiner asks

Typical style, written by us. Not copied from any paper.

  • "What is the value of x? I. x + y = 8. II. 3x + 3y = 24." (one clue twice: not enough.)
  • "Is x positive? I. x² > 0. II. x³ > 0." (II alone: a cube keeps the sign, while a square hides it.)
  • "What is the area of the rectangle? I. Perimeter is 30 cm. II. Length is 3 cm more than breadth."
  • "What is x + y? I. x − y = 1. II. 2x + 2y = 14."
How the wrong options are built. Look for the "two statements, two unknowns" bait (dependent clues), the "x² = number" bait (two roots), and the "asks for x + y but you solved x and y" detour.

9Quiz · exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official 1/4 negative rule). Guessing breaks even at a 20% chance, so guess only when your chance is above that.

10Lock it in

Cover the page, say it out loud: (1) Do two clues always beat two unknowns? (2) How many values does x² = 16 give? (3) When asked for x + y, must I find x? (4) What do I do first with "x + y = a, x − y = b"?
Memorise this
1. Count DIFFERENT clues, not statements.
2. Multiples of one clue = one clue.
3. x² = k gives plus and minus.
4. Read what is asked: x + y may be easy.
5. Add and subtract paired clues.

Sources: Math Is Fun: Systems of Linear Equations (dependent and inconsistent equations). x² = k giving ±√k, a cube keeping the sign, and the add/subtract shortcut: derived, Python-checked. Exam facts: PFRDA 2026 notification. All worked examples and the lab logic checked by Python (brute force over integer ranges).
UNVERIFIED: exact PFRDA option wording; whether the paper includes Data Sufficiency in Phase I (LOW priority: zero questions in the 2025 and 2022 memory-based counts).

Mixed Practice and Exam Technique

In one line: Quant is a 20-question game where knowing which questions to do first, which to park and when to guess is worth as much as speed.

1Picture this

You are at a Sunday market with ₹100 of time. Some stalls (quadratic equations, number series) sell you a mark in 30 seconds. Others (a heavy DI set) take four minutes. A wise buyer visits the cheap stalls first and does not stand for ten minutes haggling at one.

Do-first questions · ~7 minRest · ~5 min

Suggested plan, not official: 12 minutes for 20 questions is 36 seconds each on average. The paper is 60 minutes for four sections (official).

2The routine, one tiny step at a time

Read the question once. Within about 10 seconds, label it Do, Park or Drop. This is a suggested rule, not an official one.
Do = you know the routine (for example a quadratic pair, a number series with an obvious gap).
Park = you know the topic but it needs a long calculation. Come back after the Do pile.
Drop = you do not know the topic. Guess only if you can cross out at least one option (see the lab below).
Before exact arithmetic, estimate. Cross out options that are far from your estimate.
For a DI set: look at the table once, then answer the easiest two or three questions of that set first.
After every mock, fill the error log (card 7). The log is how you get better, not the score.

3The rules (now that you get it)

Marks: +1.25 for a right answer, −0.3125 for a wrong one, 0 for a skip (official).
Two-touch rule (suggested): if you have worked on a question twice and are still not near any option, park it.
Elimination rule (suggested): estimate first; strike every option that is more than your rounding error away.

Marking scheme: PFRDA 2026 notification (pfrda.org.in/documents/33652/212847/Recruitment+of+Officer+Grade+A+(Assistant+Manager)-2026.pdf, pp.6-7). The two rules above are our own coaching suggestions, not exam rules.

4Worked example: estimation eliminates options

38% of 1,210 is closest to: 360, 460, 560, 660?

Step 1. Round: 38% is close to 40%; 1,210 is close to 1,200.
Step 2. 10% of 1,200 = 120, so 40% = 480.
Step 3. 38% is a bit less than 40%, so the answer is a bit less than 480.
Step 4. Only 460 is a bit below 480. 360 is too low, 560 and 660 are too high. Exact check: 0.38 × 1,210 = 459.8.

Skip decision: a block of 5 questions. Suppose the average time budget is 36 s per question.

Step 1. Block budget = 5 × 36 s = 180 s = 3 minutes.
Step 2. If one question has used 4 minutes (240 s), it has eaten 240 ÷ 36 ≈ 6.7 questions' worth of time.
Step 3. Park it earlier. The marks from that one question (1.25) are the same as an easy one.

5The trap

You are on a long division and you "almost have it". You have spent 4 minutes.
Wrong move: "I have come this far, I must finish." That 4 minutes could have bought about 6 average questions (240 ÷ 36 = 6.7).
Right: write the question number in your mind as "Parked", move on, and come back only after the Do pile is empty.

Second trap: guessing blind with 5 options has expected gain exactly 0 (see the lab). It only pays once an option is crossed out.

6Choosing the method in 5 seconds

Each wording cue points to one method and one chapter. Learn cues, not whole solutions.

If you see...Do thisChapter
'x' and 'y' equationsFactorise, list roots, compare all pairs13
'from ... to ...' percent(New − Old) ÷ Old4
Two investors, monthsCapital × time ratio5
Average with remove/addTotals, not averages6
Platform / pole / crossingm/s, add lengths16
Series with a gapDifferences, then ratios, then squares12
Table with 'approximately'Round, then eliminate11, 19

Cue drill

Say the method out loud before you tap Show.

9Quiz · two mini mocks, exam-style marks

Right = +1.25, wrong = −0.3125 (official). Each set has 20 questions in 4 blocks of 5. Do one block, then stop and rest. Try the Do / Park / Drop labels while you go. Data in the tables is invented.

Option count: most questions have 4 options (A to D). The 5 quadratic questions (Blocks A2 and A3) use the usual 5-option format: A x > y, B x < y, C x ≥ y, D x ≤ y, E no relation. Pick the strongest relation that holds for every pair: if x > y always holds, choose A, not C (C is true but weaker). Choose E only when no option holds for every pair.
